Recursive inseparability of classical theories of a binary predicate and non-classical logics of a unary predicate Thanks: The work is supported by the Basic Research Program of the HSE University.
Mikhail Rybakov

Abstract
The paper considers algorithmic properties of classical and non-classical first-order logics and theories in bounded languages. The main idea is to prove the undecidability of various fragments of classical and non-classical first-order logics and theories indirectly — by extracting it as a consequence of the recursive inseparability of special problems associated with them. First, we propose a domino problem, which makes it possible to catch the recursive inseparability of two sets. Second, using this problem, we prove that the classical first-order logic of a binary predicate and the theory of its finite models where the predicate is symmetric and irreflexive are recursively inseparable in a language with a single binary predicate letter and three variables (without constants and equality). Third, we prove, for an infinite class of logics, that the monadic fragment of a modal predicate logic and the logic of the class of its finite Kripke frames are recursively inseparable in languages with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables; the same result is obtained if we replace the condition of finiteness of frames with the condition of finiteness of domains allowed in frames. Forth, we expand the results to a wide class of superintuitionistic predicate logics. In particular, it is proved that the positive fragments of the intuitionistic predicate logic and the logic of the class of finite intuitionistic Kripke frames are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables. The technique used and the results obtained allow us to answer some additional questions about the decidability of special monadic fragments of some modal and superintuitionistic predicate logics.



1
Introduction

1.1
Issues under consideration

We consider algorithmic properties of classical and non-classical first-order logics and theories in restricted languages. It is well known that the classical predicate logic
\mathbf{QCl}
is undecidable
[
8
]
, and to prove this, it is sufficient to use a single binary predicate letter and three individual variables
[
60
, Section 4.8 (ii)]
(see also
[
58
]
for three variables and
[
3
, Chapter 21]
for a single binary predicate letter); the same is true for many classical theories
[
9
,
57
,
38
,
39
,
41
]
. At the same time, the monadic fragment of
\mathbf{QCl}
is decidable. The decidability remains if we add the equality
[
3
, Chapter 21]
, allow only formulas with at most two variables
[
28
,
15
]
or use only formulas of guarded fragments
[
13
]
. In general, the classical decision problem
[
4
]
has been transformed into a classification problem when the purpose of the study is to search for boundaries within which the decidability or undecidability is still preserved. As for non-classical logics, they are usually undecidable even if the language contains only monadic predicate letters
[
24
,
26
,
33
,
37
]
or two individual variables
[
22
]
or even both a single monadic letter and two-three individual variables
[
43
,
44
,
47
]
; known results on decidability are obtained under fairly strong restrictions on language or semantics
[
18
,
19
,
67
,
68
,
37
,
49
,
1
]
.
In this paper, some general methods for obtaining results on the undecidability of fragments of classical and non-classical logics and theories will be proposed. With their help, we will get answers to some questions.

The paper is based on three questions, and our purpose is, in particular, to answer them. In addition, there are several other issues that have also been resolved.

The first question was posed by Evgeny Zolin in 2019 during a conference and consists of the following:

•

Are the monadic fragments of modal predicate logics defined by classes of finite Kripke frames decidable in languages with finitely many individual variables?

The question is answered only partially. So, it is proved that the monadic fragment of a logic defined by a finite Kripke frame is decidable
[
37
]
; the same is true for every logic
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}
, where
n\in\mathds{N}
,
1
1

1

We use
\mathds{N}
,
\mathds{N}^{+}
, and
\mathds{N}^{++}
for, respectively, the sets of natural numbers including
0
, positive natural numbers, and positive natural numbers without
1
.
defined as a logic of the class of Kripke frames where each world sees at most
n
worlds
[
37
]
, even if we add the equality to the language
[
49
]
. Also, it is proved that the monadic fragments of logics defined by the classes of finite Kripke frames of logics such as
\mathbf{QK}
,
\mathbf{QT}
,
\mathbf{QD}
,
\mathbf{QKB}
,
\mathbf{QKTB}
,
\mathbf{QK4}
,
\mathbf{QS4}
, and some others are undecidable in languages with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables
[
44
]
; similar results are obtained for superintuitionistic predicate logics
[
47
]
. It should be noted that the methods used in
[
44
,
47
]
give us nothing about logics such as
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
,
\mathbf{QK4B}
and logics containing formulas bounding the depth of Kripke frames;
in addition, they do not cover fragments with two individual variables.
As a result, we must admit that the question has not received a satisfactory answer. We shall fix it.

The second question initially stimulated the author’s interest and was later posed by Stanislav Speranski during a private conversation in 2022, concerning some broader, more general issues:

•

Is the classical theory of finite models for the symmetric irreflexive binary relation decidable in a language with a single binary predicate letter and three individual variables?

It is known that the theory of the symmetric irreflexive binary relation is undecidable
[
29
,
23
,
57
]
and even in a language with a single binary predicate letter and three individual variables (without constants and equality)
[
38
]
. Although the methods of
[
38
]
allow us to prove undecidability for many classical theories of a binary predicate, in particular, defined by classes of finite models, they are not directly applicable to the theory asked. Notice that if the fragment of this theory is undecidable, then we can readily answer the question on the decidability of logics defined by finite Kripke frames of
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
, or
\mathbf{QK4B}
in languages with a single unary predicate letter and three variables.

The third question was posed by Valentin Shehtman during a conference in 2023, and was as follows:

•

Are the monadic fragment of a modal predicate logic and the monadic fragment of the logic of its finite Kripke frames recursively separable?

This question provided a broader perspective on the situation, based on the insights gained from the previous two questions.
For the classical first-order logic, the Trakhtenbrot theorem
[
61
,
62
]
says that
\mathbf{QCl}
and the theory of finite models are recursively inseparable and, as a corollary, undecidable. Thus, this theorem shows us another way of proving the undecidability. We are going to go this way to answer all the questions raised (and not only).

First of all, we answer the question of Stanislav Speranski. To this end, we propose a tiling problem allowing us to catch recursive inseparability, and then describe it by first-order formulas with a single binary predicate letter and three individual variables; as a result, we obtain a proof of the Trakhtenbrot theorem for this language. After this is done, we modify the construction so that it can be expanded to the theory of symmetric irreflexive binary predicate. Then we use the results to answer the question of Valentin Shehtman and, as a corollary, to the question of Evgeny Zolin in the case of three variables in the language. Along the way, we get the answers to some close questions. In particular, we answer a question similar to that posed by Valentin Shehtman:

•

Are the monadic fragment of a modal predicate logic and the monadic fragment of the logic of its frames with finite domains recursively separable?

Also, using the technique of
[
22
]
, we then show that for many logics the number of variables used to prove the recursive inseparability of certain problems can be reduced to two, which gives us a more exhaustive answer to the question of Evgeny Zolin.

In addition, we answer similar questions for the intuitionistic predicate logic
\mathbf{QInt}
and some of its extensions. So, it is known that the intuitionistic predicate logic is undecidable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables
[
43
]
and the logic of the class of finite intuitionistic frames is undecidable (even not recursively enumerable) in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables
[
47
]
.
Below, we get the answer to the following question:

•

Are the intuitionistic predicate logic and the logic of its finite Kripke frames recursively separable in the language containing a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables?

We shall show that the answer is “No” even for the positive fragments; and the same for the intuitionistic predicate logic and the logic of its frames with finite domains. It is not difficult to show that the intuitionistic predicate logic can be replaced by any superintuitionistic predicate logic contained in
\mathbf{QKC}
, the predicate logic of the weak law of the excluded middle,
without affecting the truth of the results.
This is so since the positive fragments of
\mathbf{QInt}
and
\mathbf{QKC}
coincide. It should be noted that this observation was significantly used in earlier works
[
43
,
47
]
, where the undecidability of the monadic fragments was obtained only for the superintuitionistic logics contained in
\mathbf{QKC}
. Thus, the following question remained unanswered:

•

Is there a superintuitionistic predicate logic not contained in
\mathbf{QKC}
such that every its superintuitionistic sublogic is undecidable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables?

Using an approach based on obtaining the recursive inseparability of problems, we show that there are infinitely many such logics. Moreover, for each of them, the logic and the logic of its finite Kripke frames are recursively inseparable in a language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables.

Another goal of the author is to obtain very simple, clear, and short proofs of the results obtained. This seems important because earlier papers containing solutions to close issues often also contain too many technical details. The abundance of such details makes it difficult to identify essential points in order to use them in other studies, and also creates certain difficulties for including the results obtained in the educational process. With this in mind, the reader will find quite a few simple statements below, leading step by step to solving the questions posed.
The results were presented in
[
40
]
.

1.2
Short explanation of the technical part

The technical basis of the constructions used below is made up of well-known ideas that interact and intertwine in a certain way. So, we use Turing machines, tiling problems, relativization, translations, embeddings, and semantical methods. Now we pay attention only to the ideas of simulating predicate letters in the modal first-order language by formulas with a single unary predicate letter.

In earlier author’s papers (including joint works) considering issues of algorithmic complexity of non-classical logics of a unary predicate, some ideas originating in works related to the complexity of propositional logics
[
2
,
56
,
16
,
6
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
were used in a straightforward way
[
33
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
47
,
46
,
31
]
. This led to the fact that in order to prove that the fragment of a logic is undecidable, first an undecidable problem was modeled in it using an unlimited set of unary predicate letters (originally, propositional variables), and then all these predicate letters were simulated by formulas containing a single unary predicate letter (originally, a single propositional variable). So, to simulate propositional variables
p_{1},\ldots,p_{n}
, formulas like
\Diamond^{k}p
, where
1\leqslant k\leqslant n
, were used (slightly more complicated, but it does not matter now) and to simulate
P_{k}(x)
, an analog like
\Diamond^{k}P(x)
was taken.
Making such a choice facilitated the transfer of techniques from the field of propositional logic to the field of predicate logic quite easily.
But we had to pay for this choice, for example, by losing the logics containing formulas bounding the depth of Kripke frames. We are going to win them back.

\vdots
\cdots
\cdots
\Longrightarrow
w
the domain of
w
w
the domain of
w

Figure 1:
Simulating worlds by individuals

The idea is to move some simulating constructions from the modal predicate language into its classical fragment. For the semantic part of such modeling, this means that we replace some structures formed by possible worlds with structures formed by elements in the domain of a frame. For example, the formula
\Diamond^{k}P(x)
, being true at a world
w
of a Kripke model, says that there exists a sequence of
k+1
worlds starting with
w
, and its last element satisfies
P(x)
. We can say something similar using a binary predicate letter
R
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi_{k}(x)&=&\exists x_{1}\ldots\exists x_{k}\,(R(x,x_{1})\wedge R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge\ldots\wedge R(x_{k-1},x_{k})\wedge P(x_{k})),\end{array}

see Figure
1
. The last formula is modality-free, but it contains a binary predicate letter and
k
new individual variables. We are to eliminate them. To eliminate all except two variables, we can just alternate the variables. So, the formula

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi_{3}(x)&=&\exists x_{1}\exists x_{2}\exists x_{3}\,(R(x,x_{1})\wedge R(x_{1},x_{2})\wedge R(x_{2},x_{3})\wedge P(x_{3}))\end{array}

is equivalent in
\mathbf{QCl}
to the formula

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi^{\prime}_{3}(x)&=&\exists y\,(R(x,y)\wedge\exists x\,(R(y,x)\wedge\exists y\,(R(x,y)\wedge P(y))))\end{array}

containing only two individual variables; similarly for any other
k\in\mathds{N}^{+}
. To eliminate the binary predicate letter
R
, following the Kripke construction
[
24
]
, we can simulate
R(x,y)
by a formula like
\Diamond(P_{1}(x)\wedge P_{2}(y))
or even
\Diamond(P(x)\wedge\Diamond P(y))
. Notice that in the last case we lose extensions of
\mathbf{QT}
and logics of depth two (if the depth of a singleton is one). If
R
corresponds to a symmetric irreflexive binary relation, then formula
R(x,y)
can be simulated also by a formula like
\neg\Diamond(P(x)\wedge P(y))
or a similar formula
[
37
]
, and we do not lose that logics. Thus, to answer the questions concerning monadic fragments of logics of frames with finite domains (and, as we shall see, of classes of finite Kripke frames, too), it is useful to know properties of the theory of finite symmetric irreflexive binary predicate.

Of course, in the constructions below, there are some additional details that we have to take into account, and this example just illustrates some simple principles that we are going to use throughout the paper.

1.3
Structure of the paper

We begin with describing preliminary constructions is Section
2
we shall use throughout the paper. Namely, we shall define a convenient modification of Turing machines and then a suitable domino problem that actually describes computations of such machines.

Then, in Section
3
, we use the domino problem to prove a generalization of the Trakhtenbrot theorem for a number of theories of a binary predicate in the language containing a single binary predicate letter and three variables. Our main aim is to prove that the classical predicate logic and the theory of finite models for symmetric irreflexive binary relation are recursively inseparable in this language.

Section
4
contains corollaries of the results presented in Section
3
and additional theorems. So, using the mentioned results, we shall prove that almost all modal predicate logics and logics of their finite Kripke frames (or the ones of frames with finite domains) are recursively inseparable in the language containing a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables;
in addition, with certain exceptions, similar conclusions will be drawn
for logics in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables.

In Section
5
, we show that the same is true also for a number of superintuitionistic predicate logics and even for their positive fragments.

We make some remarks about complexity in Section
6
and then conclude in Section
7
.

2
Preliminary constructions

2.1
Turing machines

For our purposes, we define a modification of Turing machines
[
25
,
54
]
.
We consider single-tape deterministic Turing machines. Their special features are a finite set of halting states instead of one halting state and instructions beginning with halting states. These instructions work infinitely when we reach a halting state; they are useful to make the tilings we shall consider infinite.

Thus, a
Turing machine
is a tuple
M=\langle\Sigma,Q,q_{0},F,\delta\rangle
, where
\Sigma
is a finite alphabet such that
\Box,\#\in\Sigma
(
\Box
is the
blank symbol
and
\#
is the
end tape marker symbol
),
Q
is a finite set of
states
;
q_{0}\in Q
is the
initial state
;
F
is a set of
halting states
,
F\subseteq Q
; and
\delta
is a
program
. The program
\delta
is a function
\delta\colon Q\times\Sigma\to Q\times\Sigma\times\{L,S,R\}
; it satisfies some conditions defined below. If
\delta\colon{q}{s}\mapsto{q^{\prime}}{s^{\prime}}{\Delta}
, then

•

q^{\prime}\neq q_{0}
;

•

s=\#
if, and only if,
s^{\prime}=\#
;

•

\Delta\neq L
whenever
s=\#
;

•

q^{\prime}=q
,
s^{\prime}=s
, and
\Delta=S
whenever
q\in F
.

A
configuration
of a machine
M=\langle\Sigma,Q,q_{0},F,\delta\rangle
is an
\omega
-word
vqv^{\prime}
, where
q\in Q
and
vv^{\prime}=a_{0}a_{1}a_{2}\ldots{}
is an
\omega
-word over
\Sigma
satisfying the following conditions:

•

there exists
k\in\mathds{N}^{+}
such that
a_{i}=\Box
, for every
i\geqslant k
;

•

a_{i}=\#
if, and only if,
i=0
.

A Turing machine
M
can be thought of as a computing device equipped with a tape divided into an infinite sequence of cells
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2},\ldots{}
, each containing a symbol from
\Sigma
, with one cell being scanned by a movable head. Then, a configuration
vqv^{\prime}
of
M
represents a computation instant at which the tape contains the symbols of the word
vv^{\prime}
,
M
is in state
q
, and the head is scanning the cell containing the first symbol of
v^{\prime}
. An
instruction

\delta\colon{q}{s}\mapsto{q^{\prime}}{s^{\prime}}{\Delta}
is applicable to this configuration just in case
M
is in state
q
and is scanning a cell containing
s
. As a result of applying this instruction,
M
enters state
q^{\prime}
, replaces
s
with
s^{\prime}
in the cell, and either moves one cell to the left or to the right or stays put, depending on whether
\Delta
is
L
,
R
or
S
, respectively. Given a word
x
over
\Sigma\setminus\{\Box,\#\}
as an
input
,
M
consecutively executes the instructions of
\delta
starting from the configuration
q_{0}\#x\Box\Box\Box\ldots{}
; if
M
reaches a configuration whose state component
q
is in
F
, then
M

halts
on
x
, which is denoted by
q!M(x)
; if
M
does not halt on
x
, we write
\neg!M(x)
. Without a loss of generality, we may assume that the cell being scanned when
M
halts is
c_{0}
(which contains
\#
). Notice that then instruction
\delta\colon q\#\mapsto{q\#}S
can be applied providing us with the same configuration. This means that even if
M
halts, we may consider the infinite computation of
M
, in which
M
loops the same halting configuration.

We will use Turing machines as a computational model for partial recursive functions on
\mathds{N}
. To do this, we have to encode natural numbers in some finite alphabet. For
m\in\mathds{N}
, let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\overline{m}&=&\underbrace{||\ldots|}_{\mathclap{\mbox{$m$ times}}},\end{array}

i.e.,
\overline{0}
is the empty word,
\overline{1}={|}
,
\overline{2}={||}
,
\overline{3}={|||}
, etc.; the word
\overline{m}
is the
code
of
m
.

2.2
Recursive separability

Let
X
and
Y
be subsets of
\mathds{N}
such that
X\cap Y=\varnothing
. Then
X
and
Y
are called
recursively separable
if for some recursive subset
Z
of
\mathds{N}
both
X\subseteq Z
and
Y\cap Z=\varnothing
hold; if there is no such
Z
, then
X
and
Y
are called
recursively inseparable
.

We shall deal with theories and logics regarding them as special sets of formulas. Usually, theories intersect; therefore, the notion of recursive separability is not applicable directly to them (i.e., to their Gödel numbers). However, it is applicable if we consider theories
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
such that
T_{1}\subseteq T_{2}
or
T_{2}\subseteq T_{1}
. If, say,
T_{1}\subseteq T_{2}
, then we may consider
T_{1}
-validity problem and
T_{2}
-refutability problem whose intersection is, clearly, empty.

This observation leads to the following natural expansion of the definition of recursive separability. Let
X
and
Y
be subsets of
\mathds{N}
such that
X\subset Y
. Then we call
X
and
Y

recursively separable
if
X
and
\mathds{N}\setminus Y
are recursively separable, i.e.,
X\subseteq Z\subseteq Y
for some recursive
Z
; otherwise we call
X
and
Y

recursively inseparable
.

Figure 2:
Tile with marks

It is known that there exist non-intersecting recursively enumerable sets
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
which are recursively inseparable
[
30
, Theorem 3.3]
. Then there is a partial recursive function
f_{\mathbb{XY}}\colon\mathds{N}\to\mathds{N}
distinguishing
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}f_{\mathbb{XY}}(x)&=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}0&\mbox{if $x\in\mathbb{X}$;}\\
1&\mbox{if $x\in\mathbb{Y}$;}\\
\mbox{undefined}&\mbox{if $x\not\in\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{Y}$.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{array}

This function is computable by some Turing machine
M_{0}=\langle\Sigma_{0},Q_{0},q_{0},F_{0},\delta_{0}\rangle
. We may assume that
M_{0}
has two halting states
q_{\mathbb{X}}
and
q_{\mathbb{Y}}
such that, for every
m\in\mathds{N}
,

•

if
m\in\mathbb{X}
, then
q_{\mathbb{X}}!M_{0}(\overline{m})
;

•

if
m\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
q_{\mathbb{Y}}!M_{0}(\overline{m})
;

also, notice that

•

if
m\not\in\mathbb{X}\cup\mathbb{Y}
, then
\neg!M_{0}(\overline{m})
.

For further constructions, we could explicitly specify
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathbb{Y}
, and
M_{0}
explicitly; but we shall give general constructions that allow us to vary them. However, for the rest of the text, let
\mathbb{X}
,
\mathbb{Y}
, and
M_{0}
be fixed.

2.3
Tiling problem we shall consider

For our purposes, let us represent
M_{0}
by sets of square tile types — one set for every input
\overline{m}
, where
m\in\mathds{N}
. The aim is to replace the computations of
M_{0}
with
\mathds{N}\times\mathds{N}
tilings.
2
2

2

For tiling problems consult
[
17
]
.

We may think of a
tile
as a
1\times 1
square, with a fixed orientation, whose edges are marked by words over some finite alphabet (we will use
\Sigma_{0}\cup Q_{0}
enriched by some technical symbols).
A
tile type

t
consists of a specification of a
mark
(i.e., a word) for each edge; we write

Let
T=\{t_{0},\ldots,t_{n}\}
be a set of tile types. Informally, a
T
-tiling
is an arrangement of tiles, whose types are in
T
, on a grid so that the edge marks of adjacent tiles match, both horizontally and vertically. Formally, define
T
-tiling as a function
f\colon\mathds{N}\times\mathds{N}\to T
such that for all
i,j\in\mathds{N}
,

Tile types
t_{0}

and

\delta\colon qs\mapsto q^{\prime}s^{\prime}\Delta

Figure 7:
Tile types for instruction
\delta_{0}\colon qs\mapsto q^{\prime}s^{\prime}L

Let
{T}_{M_{0}}
be the set of all tile types defined above; notice that
{T}_{M_{0}}
is finite. The set
{T}_{M_{0}}
contains a description of
M_{0}
, but does not contain any tile types that allow us to simulate inputs for
M_{0}
. To fix this, define the tile types
t_{\Box}^{\ast\ast}
,
t_{k}^{\otimes}
, and
t_{k}^{\ast\ast}
, where

\overline{k}

We shall use the new tile types together with the tile types of
{T}_{M_{0}}
to simulate the computations of
M_{0}
with the inputs we choose. To this end, let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}T_{n}&=&{T}_{M_{0}}\cup\{t_{k}^{\otimes}:k<n\}\cup\{t_{n}^{\ast\ast}\}\cup\{t_{\Box}^{\ast\ast}\}.\end{array}

Proposition 2.1

There exists a unique
T_{n}
-tiling
f\colon\mathds{N}\times\mathds{N}\to T_{n}
such that
f(0,0)=t_{0}
. Moreover, if
C_{0},C_{1},C_{2},\ldots{}
is the computation of
M_{0}
on the input
\overline{n}
, then

\begin{array}[]{lcl}C_{m}&=&\mathop{\hbox to5.52pt{\vbox to5.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

for every
m\in\mathds{N}
.

Induction on
m
.

Due to Proposition
2.1
, a
T_{n}
-tiling
f_{n}\colon\mathds{N}\times\mathds{N}\to T_{n}
such that
f_{n}(0,0)=t_{0}
is defined uniquely; we call it the
special
T_{n}
-tiling
.
Let, for convenience,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}t_{1}=t_{q_{\mathbb{X}}\#}&\mbox{and}&t_{2}=t_{q_{\mathbb{Y}}\#}.\end{array}

From Proposition
2.1
we obtain that, for every
n\in\mathds{N}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}n\in\mathbb{X}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{there exists $m\in\mathds{N}$ such that $f_{n}(0,m)=t_{1}$;}\\
n\in\mathbb{Y}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{there exists $m\in\mathds{N}$ such that $f_{n}(0,m)=t_{2}$.}\\
\end{array}

(2.1)

In the following, it does not matter to us how exactly the tile types of
T_{n}
are constructed; we just enumerate them so that we could refer to
(\ref{eq:fn})
. So, let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}T_{n}&=&\{t^{n}_{0},\ldots,t^{n}_{k_{n}}\}\end{array}

and assume that

\begin{array}[]{lcl}t^{n}_{0}=t_{0}^{\phantom{n}},&t^{n}_{1}=t_{1}^{\phantom{n}},&t^{n}_{2}=t_{2}^{\phantom{n}};\end{array}

the enumeration of all other tile types of
T_{n}
is arbitrary. We shall use
T_{n}
to describe the properties
(\ref{eq:fn})
of the special
T_{n}
-tiling by formulas of different languages. This will allow us to prove that the validity and the refutability problems for certain theories and logics are recursively inseparable, and hence the theories and the logics are undecidable or even not recursively enumerable.

3
Superclassical logics and theories

3.1
Syntax and semantics

We assume that the first-order language
\mathcal{L}
contains countably many individual variables, countably many predicate letters of every arity, the constant
\bot
, the binary connectives
\wedge
,
\vee
,
\to
, the quantifier symbols
\forall
and
\exists
. Formulas in
\mathcal{L}
, or
\mathcal{L}
-formulas
, as well as the symbols
\neg
and
\leftrightarrow
, are defined in the usual way; in particular,
\neg\varphi=\varphi\to\bot
,
\top=\neg\bot
, and
\varphi\leftrightarrow\psi=(\varphi\to\psi)\wedge(\psi\to\varphi)
. From now on, we identify the language
\mathcal{L}
with the set of
\mathcal{L}
-formulas. For a formula
\varphi
, let
\mathop{\mathit{sub}}\varphi
denote the set of subformulas of
\varphi
.

A
model
is a tuple
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
, where
D
is a non-empty set of
individuals
, or the
domain
of
\bm{M}
, and
\mathcal{I}
an
interpretation of predicate letters
in
\mathcal{D}
, i.e., a function assigning to an
n
-ary
predicate letter
P
an
n
-ary
relation
\mathcal{I}(P)
on
\mathcal{D}
.

An
assignment
in a model
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
is a function
g
associating with every individual variable
x
an individual
g(x)\in\mathcal{D}
. As before,
g\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{=}}h
means that the assignment
g
differs from the assignment
h
in at most the value of
x
.

The truth of a formula
\varphi
in a model
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
under an assignment
g
is defined recursively:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{M}\models^{g}P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox{211.27805pt}{$\langle g(x_{1}),\ldots,g(x_{n})\rangle\in\mathcal{I}(P)$,}\\
\end{array}

where
P
is an
n
-ary predicate letter;

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{53.49263pt}{$\bm{M}\not\models^{g}\bot;$}}\\
\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{53.49263pt}{$\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}\wedge\varphi^{\prime\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{95.9854pt}{$\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}$ and $\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime\prime}$;}\\
\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{53.49263pt}{$\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}\vee\varphi^{\prime\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{95.9854pt}{$\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}$\hfill or\hfill$\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime\prime}$;}\\
\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{53.49263pt}{$\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}\to\varphi^{\prime\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{95.9854pt}{$\bm{M}\not\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}$\hfill or\hfill$\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime\prime}$;}\\
\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{53.49263pt}{$\bm{M}\models^{g}\forall x\,\varphi^{\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\mbox{$\bm{M}\models^{h}\varphi^{\prime}$, for every assignment $h$ such that $h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{=}}g$;}\\
\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{53.49263pt}{$\bm{M}\models^{g}\exists x\,\varphi^{\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\mbox{$\bm{M}\models^{h}\varphi^{\prime}$, for some assignment $h$ such that $h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{=}}g$.}\end{array}

For a formula
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
with its free individual variables in the list
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
and individuals
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}
of
\bm{M}
, we write
\bm{M}\models\varphi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
if
\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, for an assignment
g
such that
g(x_{k})=a_{k}
, for every
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
. This notation is unambiguous since the languages we consider lack constants and the truth value of
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
does not depend on the values of variables different from
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.

For a model
\bm{M}
, a class
\mathscr{C}
of models, a formula
\varphi
, and a set of formulas
X
, define

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{31.90958pt}{$\bm{M}\models\varphi$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{159.50017pt}{$\bm{M}\models^{g}\varphi$, for every assignment\penalty\ $g$;}\\
\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{31.90958pt}{$\bm{M}\models X$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{159.50017pt}{$\bm{M}\models\varphi$, for every $\varphi\in X$;}\\
\parbox{76.38393pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{31.90958pt}{$\parbox{12.56244pt}{\hfill$\mathscr{C}$}\models\varphi$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{159.50017pt}{$\bm{M}\models\varphi$, for every $\bm{M}\in\mathscr{C}$.}\\
\end{array}

If
\bm{M}\models\varphi
, we say that
\varphi
is
true
in
\bm{M}
; otherwise
\varphi
is
refuted
in
\bm{M}
.

We say that a model
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
is
finite
if
\mathcal{D}
is finite.

For a class
\mathscr{C}
of models, define
\mathit{Th}(\mathscr{C})=\{\varphi:\mathscr{C}\models\varphi\}
. We call
\mathit{Th}(\mathscr{C})
the
theory
of the class
\mathscr{C}
.
For a theory
\Gamma
, let
\Gamma_{\mathit{fin}}
denote the theory of all finite models of
\Gamma
.
A model
\bm{M}
is a
\Gamma
-model
if
\bm{M}\models\Gamma
.
For a formula
\varphi
, let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\Gamma\models\varphi&\mathrel{\leftrightharpoons}&\mbox{$\bm{M}\models\varphi$, for every $\Gamma$-model $\bm{M}$;}\\
\Gamma\uplus\varphi&=&\{\psi\in\mathcal{L}:\Gamma\cup\{\varphi\}\models\psi\}.\end{array}

Below we do not differ
\Gamma
and
\Gamma\uplus\top
(i.e.,
\{\psi\in\mathcal{L}:\Gamma\models\psi\}
).

A closed
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi
is called

•

\Gamma
-valid
if
\Gamma\models\varphi
;

•

\Gamma
-satisfiable
, or
\Gamma
-consistent
, if
\Gamma\not\models\neg\varphi
;

•

\Gamma
-refutable
if
\Gamma\not\models\varphi
.

Define logics
\mathbf{QCl}
and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
as, respectively, the theory of the class of all models and the theory of the class of all finite models.

3.2
Trakhtenbrot theorem for three variables

The Trakhtenbrot theorem
[
61
,
62
]
states that
\mathbf{QCl}
and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable (or equivalently,
\mathbf{QCl}
-validity and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
-refutability problems are).
We give a very short proof of the theorem and then obtain corollaries that relate theories of a binary predicate. The proof is based on the simulation of the special
T_{n}
-tiling, for every
n\in\mathds{N}
, by
\mathcal{L}
-formulas. To this end, we will use a binary predicate letter
P
to describe some connections between the elements of the models (the elements are viewed as tile holders) and unary letters
P_{0},P_{1},P_{2},\ldots{}
to say, tiles of which types are held by the elements.

Using them, define formulas describing conditions for the special
T_{n}
-tiling:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{TC}_{0}&=&\displaystyle\forall x\,\bigvee\limits_{i=0}^{k_{n}}\Big(P_{i}(x)\wedge\bigwedge\limits_{j\neq i}\neg P_{j}(x)\Big);\\
\mathit{TC}_{1}&=&\forall x\exists y\,H_{n}(x,y);\\
\mathit{TC}_{2}&=&\forall x\exists y\,V_{n}(x,y);\\
\mathit{TC}_{3}&=&\forall x\forall y\,(\exists z\,(H_{n}(x,z)\wedge V_{n}(z,y))\leftrightarrow\exists z\,(V_{n}(x,z)\wedge H_{n}(z,y)));\\
\mathit{TC}_{4}&=&\exists x\,P_{0}(x).\end{array}

Then, we can say that we are given the special
T_{n}
-tiling:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}&=&\mathit{TC}_{0}\wedge\mathit{TC}_{1}\wedge\mathit{TC}_{2}\wedge\mathit{TC}_{3}\wedge\mathit{TC}_{4}.\end{array}

Also, it is easy to say that
n\in\mathbb{X}
or that
n\in\mathbb{Y}
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}&=&\mathit{Tiling}_{n}\to\exists x\,P_{1}(x);\\
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{Y}}&=&\mathit{Tiling}_{n}\to\exists x\,P_{2}(x).\end{array}

In fact, to prove the Trakhtenbrot theorem, we need just one of these formulas. To obtain the proof, it is sufficient to make two observations concerning any (say, the first) of them; we present the observations in two lemmas below.

Lemma 3.1

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QCl}
.

Let
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
be a model such that
\bm{M}\models\mathit{Tiling}_{n}
. We show that then
\bm{M}\models\exists x\,P_{1}(x)
.
To this end, for all
i,j\in\mathds{N}
we pick out an element
a_{i}^{j}\in\mathcal{D}
so that, for all
i,j\in\mathds{N}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{M}\models H_{n}(a_{i}^{j},a_{i+1}^{j})&\mbox{and}&\bm{M}\models V_{n}(a_{i}^{j},a_{i}^{j+1}).\end{array}

Since
\bm{M}\models\mathit{TC}_{4}
, there exists
a_{0}^{0}\in D
such that
\bm{M}\models P_{0}(a_{0}^{0})
.

Let
k
be a number from
\mathds{N}
. Suppose that for all
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, the element
a_{i}^{j}
is defined; we have to define, for all
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, the elements
a_{k+1}^{j}
,
a_{i}^{k+1}
, and
a_{k+1}^{k+1}
.

We start with
a_{k+1}^{k+1}
. Due to
\mathit{TC}_{1}
and
\mathit{TC}_{2}
, there are
b,c\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\bm{M}\models H_{n}(a_{k}^{k},b)\wedge V_{n}(b,c)
. Then take
a_{k+1}^{k+1}=c
. Also, let
a_{k+1}^{k}=b
.
Due to
\mathit{TC}_{3}
, there is
b^{\prime}\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\bm{M}\models V_{n}(a_{k}^{k},b^{\prime})\wedge H_{n}(b^{\prime},c)
; let
a_{k}^{k+1}=b^{\prime}
.

Let
a_{k+1}^{j+1}
be defined so that
\bm{M}\models V_{n}(a_{k}^{j},a_{k}^{j+1})\wedge H_{n}(a_{k}^{j+1},a_{k+1}^{j+1})
. Due to
\mathit{TC}_{3}
, there is
b\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\bm{M}\models H_{n}(a_{k}^{j},b^{\prime})\wedge V_{n}(b^{\prime},a_{k+1}^{j+1})
; let
a_{k+1}^{j}=b
. This allows us to define
a_{k+1}^{j}
, for every
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.

Let
a_{i+1}^{k+1}
be defined so that
\bm{M}\models H_{n}(a_{i}^{k},a_{i+1}^{k})\wedge V_{n}(a_{i+1}^{k},a_{i+1}^{k+1})
. Due to
\mathit{TC}_{3}
, there is
b\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\bm{M}\models V_{n}(a_{i}^{k},b^{\prime})\wedge H_{n}(b^{\prime},a_{i+1}^{k+1})
; let
a_{i}^{k+1}=b
. This allows us to define
a_{i}^{k+1}
, for every
i\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
.

For any
i,j\in\mathds{N}
, due to
\mathit{TC}_{0}
, there exists a unique
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
such that
\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a_{i}^{j})
. Then, put
f(i,j)=t^{n}_{m}
.
It should be clear that
f\colon\mathds{N}\times\mathds{N}\to T_{n}
is a
T_{n}
-tiling. Indeed, the conditions
(1)
and
(2)
hold:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{M}\models H_{n}(a_{i}^{j},a_{i+1}^{j})&\Longrightarrow&\mathop{\hbox to5.52pt{\vbox to5.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 7.09 L 7.09 7.09 L 7.09 0 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.09 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f(i+1,j);\\
\bm{M}\models\hfill V_{n}(a_{i}^{j},a_{i}^{j+1})&\Longrightarrow&\mathop{\hbox to5.52pt{\vbox to5.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 7.09 L 7.09 7.09 L 7.09 0 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f(i,j)=\mathop{\hbox to5.52pt{\vbox to5.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f(i,j+1).\end{array}

Notice that
f(0,0)=t^{n}_{0}=t_{0}
since
\bm{M}\models P_{0}(a_{0}^{0})
. Then, Proposition
2.1
guarantees that
f
is the special
T_{n}
-tiling
f_{n}
. Since
n\in\mathbb{X}
, by
({\ast})
we obtain that there exists
m\in\mathds{N}
such that
f_{n}(0,m)=t_{1}=t^{n}_{1}
. This means that
\bm{M}\models P_{1}(a_{0}^{m})
, and hence,
\bm{M}\models\exists x\,P_{1}(x)
.

Thus,
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QCl}
.

Lemma 3.2

If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Y}
. By
({\ast})
, there exists
m\in\mathds{N}
such that
f_{n}(0,m)=t_{2}
. Then
M_{0}
loops the halting configuration, and, for all
i,j\in\mathds{N}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}f_{n}(i,m+j)&=&f_{n}(0,m).\end{array}

Let
r=\max\{m,n+1\}
.
Since the head of a Turing machine cannot scan cells numbered grater than the number of steps in the computation (i.e., greater than
m
) and every cell numbered grater than
n
contains
\Box
at the initial configuration, we conclude that, for all
i,j\in\mathds{N}^{+}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}f_{n}(r+i,0)&=&t^{\ast\ast}_{\Box};\\
f_{n}(r+i,j)&=&t^{\ast}_{\Box}.\end{array}

Thus, we can describe the special
T_{n}
-tiling by a finite model. Let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{D}&=&\{0,\ldots,r+2\}\times\{0,\ldots,r+2\}.\\
\end{array}

Define a model
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{M}\models P(\langle i,j\rangle,\langle i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\rangle)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{either $i^{\prime}=i+1$ and $j^{\prime}=j$,}\\
&&\mbox{or $i^{\prime}=i$ and $j^{\prime}=j+1$,}\\
&&\mbox{or $i=r+2$, $i^{\prime}=r+1$, and $j^{\prime}=j$,}\\
&&\mbox{or $i^{\prime}=i$, $j=r+2$, and $j^{\prime}=r+1$;}\\
\bm{M}\models P_{k}(\langle i,j\rangle)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&f_{n}(i,j)=t_{k}^{n},\end{array}

see Figure
10
.

Then, by the definition of
\bm{M}
, we obtain
\bm{M}\models\mathit{Tiling}_{n}
. Since
n\not\in\mathbb{X}
, there is no
i,j\in\mathds{N}
such that
f_{n}(i,j)=t_{1}
, therefore,
\bm{M}\not\models\exists x\,P_{1}(x)
.

Hence,
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

Then, we readily obtain the Trakhtenbrot theorem.

Theorem 3.3
(Trakhtenbrot)

Logics
\mathbf{QCl}
and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter, an infinite supply of unary predicate letters, and three individual variables.

Immediate from Lemmas
3.1
and
3.2
.

Below, we refine this statement and expand it on a lot of theories of a binary predicate.

3.3
Binary relation and three variables

We show how to eliminate all unary predicate letters by simulating them with formulas containing the binary letter
P
. First of all, let us make a relativization. Let
G
be a new unary predicate letter; define the quantifiers
\forall_{G}
and
\exists_{G}

by

\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\forall_{G}x\,\varphi&=&\forall x\,(G(x)\to\varphi);\\
\exists_{G}x\,\varphi&=&\exists x\,(G(x)\hfill\wedge\hfill\varphi).\end{array}

For an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi
, denote by
\varphi_{G}
the formula obtained from
\varphi
by replacing each quantifier
\forall x
or
\exists x
with
\forall_{G}x
or
\exists_{G}x
, respectively.

The following lemma is obvious.

Lemma 3.4

Let
\varphi
be a closed
\mathcal{L}
-formula without occurrences of
G
. Then

\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\varphi\in\mathbf{QCl}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\exists x\,G(x)\to\varphi_{G}\in\mathbf{QCl};\\
\varphi\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\exists x\,G(x)\to\varphi_{G}\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}.\end{array}

We give a sketch of the proof, leaving the details to the reader.

Let
\bm{M}\not\models\varphi
, for some model
\bm{M}
. Modify
\bm{M}
to
\bm{M}^{\prime}
so that
\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\forall x\,G(x)
. Then, clearly,
\bm{M}^{\prime}\not\models\exists x\,G(x)\to\varphi_{G}
.

Let
\bm{M}\not\models\exists x\,G(x)\to\varphi_{G}
, for some model
\bm{M}
. Take the submodel
\bm{M}^{\prime}
of
\bm{M}
formed by the elements of
\bm{M}
on which
G
is true. Such elements exist since
\bm{M}\models\exists x\,G(x)
. Then, clearly,
\bm{M}^{\prime}\not\models\varphi
.
Notice that if
\bm{M}
is finite, then
\bm{M}^{\prime}
is finite, too.

Let us define formulas we shall use to simulate the unary predicate letters:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\varepsilon^{y}(x)&=&\neg\exists y\,P(x,y);\\
\tau_{0}^{y}(x)&=&\exists y\,(\neg G(y)\wedge P(x,y)\wedge\varepsilon^{x}(y));\\
\tau_{k+1}^{y}(x)&=&\exists y\,(\neg G(y)\wedge P(x,y)\wedge\tau_{k}^{x}(y));\\
\mathit{tile}_{k}(x)&=&\tau_{k}^{y}(x);\\
\mathit{tile}_{k}(y)&=&\tau_{k}^{x}(y);\\
\mathit{tile}_{k}(z)&=&\tau_{k}^{x}(z),\\
\end{array}

where
k\in\mathds{N}
, and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\gamma^{y}(x)&=&\neg P(x,x)\wedge\exists y\,(P(x,y)\wedge P(y,y));\\
\mathit{grid}(x)&=&\gamma^{y}(x);\\
\mathit{grid}(y)&=&\gamma^{x}(y);\\
\hskip 20.00003pt\mathit{grid}(z)&=&\gamma^{x}(z).\\
\end{array}

Finally, let us define a function
S_{0}
associating with each formula
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
a special formula that contains no predicate letters except the binary letter
P
. Define
S_{0}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
to be obtained from
\exists x\,G(x)\to(\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}^{\phantom{i}}
by replacing each occurrence of
P_{m}(x)
,
P_{m}(y)
, and
P_{m}(z)
with
\mathit{tile}_{m}(x)
,
\mathit{tile}_{m}(y)
, and
\mathit{tile}_{m}(z)
, respectively, where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
, and then each occurrence of
G(x)
,
G(y)
, and
G(z)
with
\mathit{grid}(x)
,
\mathit{grid}(y)
, and
\mathit{grid}(z)
, respectively. Notice that these replacements are formula substitutions
3
3

3

Such substitutions are known as
indirect
substitutions: substituting formulas we use formulas that are congruent to those we substitute.
and contain three individual variables.

Lemma 3.5

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
S_{0}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QCl}
.

Follows from Lemmas
3.1
and
3.4
taking into account that
\mathbf{QCl}
is closed by Substitution.

Lemma 3.6

If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
S_{0}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Y}
. By Lemma
3.2
,
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
, and by Lemma
3.4
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\exists x\,G(x)\to(\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}^{\phantom{i}}&\not\in&\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}},\end{array}

hence, there exists a finite model
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
such that

\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{M}\penalty\ \not\models\penalty\ \exists x\,G(x)\to(\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}^{\phantom{i}}.\end{array}

Without a loss of generality we may assume that
\bm{M}\models\forall x\,G(x)
.
We extend
\bm{M}
to a bigger finite model refuting the formula
S_{0}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
.

\vdots
\vdots
\vdots
a
e_{\ast}^{a}
e_{m}^{a}
e_{1}^{a}
e_{0}^{a}

Figure 11:
Simulation of
P_{m}(a)
and
G(a)

For every
a\in\mathcal{D}
such that
\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a)
, for some
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
, add to
\mathcal{D}
new elements
e_{\ast}^{a},e_{0}^{a},\ldots,e_{m}^{a}
and extend
\mathcal{I}(P)
with
\langle a,e_{\ast}^{a}\rangle,\langle e_{\ast}^{a},e_{\ast}^{a}\rangle,\langle a,e_{m}^{a}\rangle,\langle e_{m}^{a},e_{m-1}^{a}\rangle,\ldots,\langle e_{1}^{a},e_{0}^{a}\rangle
; let
\bm{M}^{\prime}=\langle\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\rangle
be the resulting model; see Figure
11
. By the definition of
\bm{M}^{\prime}
, for every
e\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\phantom{\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a)}\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{grid}(e)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$e\in\mathcal{D}$}\\
\end{array}

and, for every
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
and every
a\in\mathcal{D}
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\phantom{\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{grid}(e)}\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{tile}_{m}(a).\end{array}

Using these, we obtain that
\bm{M}^{\prime}\not\models S_{0}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
. Notice that model
\bm{M}^{\prime}
is finite.
Thus,
S_{0}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

As a result, we obtain the following refinement of the Trakhtenbrot theorem.

Theorem 3.7

Logics
\mathbf{QCl}
and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Immediate from Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
.

But our aim is not Theorem
3.7
; we shall prove a similar statement for some special theories of a binary predicate
by adapting
constructions from the proof of the theorem.

3.4
Expanding Trakhtenbrot theorem on other theories

In fact, Theorem
3.7
says that the graph theory and the theory of finite graphs are recursively inseparable in the language with three variables (and a binary letter corresponding to the edges of graphs).
Note that we can impose additional conditions on theories, for example, by requiring graphs to be planar, bipartite, or connected. Despite these additional limitations, the theorem remains true due to the properties of the finite models used to refute

S_{0}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
with
n\in\mathbb{Y}
.
In fact, we can add some other conditions, claiming graphs to be also antisymmetric, serial, transitive or intransitive, etc.; it is possible to use ideas from
[
38
]
. But we are interested in two theories not covered by the results of
[
38
]
: the theory
\mathbf{SIB}
of a symmetric irreflexive binary relation and the theory
\mathbf{SRB}
of a symmetric reflexive binary relation. Both theories are undecidable in the language with three variables
[
38
]
but the methods of
[
38
]
do not give anything about the decidability of
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
. We shall show that
\mathbf{SIB}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable and, as a corollary,
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
is not recursively enumerable; similarly for
\mathbf{SRB}
and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
.
This result will also be very useful for solving some problems posed in modal and superintuitionistic predicate logics.

We shall deal with
\mathbf{SIB}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
; the results for
\mathbf{SRB}
and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
will be obtained as a corollary. Notice that
\mathbf{SIB}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are the theories of simple graphs, all and finite, respectively. Also,
\mathbf{SIB}
is a fragment of the theory
\mathbf{QCl}\uplus\bm{sib}
, there

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{sib}&=&\forall x\forall y\,(P(x,y)\to P(y,x))\wedge\forall x\,\neg P(x,x)\end{array}

and the language of
\mathbf{SIB}
contains
P
.

To prove an analogue of Theorem
3.7
for
\mathbf{SIB}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
, we have to solve the following problems:

•

We have to redefine
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
, where
n\in\mathds{N}
. If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QCl}\uplus\bm{sib}
since
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QCl}
by Lemma
3.2
, therefore, an analogue of Lemma
3.2
for
\mathbf{QCl}\uplus\bm{sib}
holds. But if
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then we cannot guarantee that there exists a finite model of
\mathbf{QCl}\uplus\bm{sib}
refuting
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
. This is because now
H_{n}(a,b)
and
H_{n}(b,a)
, for some elements
a
and
b
of a model, may lead to a contradiction with
\mathit{TC}_{2}\wedge\mathit{TC}_{3}
. As a result, an analog to Lemma
3.2
for
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}\uplus\bm{sib}
fails.

•

If it works, we still have to redefine
\mathit{tile}_{k}(x)
, where
k\in\mathds{N}
. The formula
\mathit{tile}_{k}(x)
says that it is possible to reach a final element in
k+1
steps; but this is impossible since the final elements of a symmetric relation are singletons.
Throughout the process, it is crucial for us to redefine
\mathit{tile}_{k}(x)
using only two variables.

Let us take new unary predicate letters
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
,
R_{3}
,
R_{4}
and
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
,
U_{3}
,
U_{4}
. We shall use
R_{1}
,
R_{2}
,
R_{3}
,
R_{4}
to simulate a directed “horizontal”
P
-shift (to the right) and
U_{1}
,
U_{2}
,
U_{3}
,
U_{4}
to simulate a directed “vertical”
P
-shift (upward). We define the following directed
P
-shift conditions:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{DSR}&=&\displaystyle\forall x\,\Big(\bigvee\limits_{\mathclap{i=1}}^{4}\big(R_{i}(x)\wedge\bigwedge\limits_{\mathclap{j\neq i}}\neg R_{j}(x)\big)\Big);\\
\mathit{DSU}&=&\displaystyle\forall x\,\Big(\bigvee\limits_{\mathclap{i=1}}^{4}\big(U_{i}(x)\hfill\wedge\hfill\bigwedge\limits_{\mathclap{j\neq i}}\neg U_{j}(x)\big)\Big).\\
\end{array}

Let also

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{RD}(x,y)&=&(R_{1}(x)\wedge R_{2}(y))\vee(R_{2}(x)\wedge R_{3}(y))\vee(R_{3}(x)\wedge R_{4}(y))\vee(R_{4}(x)\wedge R_{1}(y));\\
\mathit{UD}(x,y)&=&(U_{1}(x)\hfill\wedge\hfill U_{2}(y))\hfill\vee\hfill(U_{2}(x)\hfill\wedge\hfill U_{3}(y))\hfill\vee\hfill(U_{3}(x)\hfill\wedge\hfill U_{4}(y))\hfill\vee\hfill(U_{4}(x)\hfill\wedge\hfill U_{1}(y)).\\
\end{array}

Informally,
\mathit{RD}(x,y)
means that
x
and
y
can be viewed as candidates for
P
-moving in the right direction from
x
to
y
; similarly,
\mathit{UD}(x,y)
means that
x
and
y
can be viewed as candidates for
P
-moving in the upward direction from
x

Figure 12:
“Hidden” tiling built-in the special
T_{n}
-tiling

Next, we redefine the formulas
H_{n}(x,y)
,
V_{n}(x,y)
,
\mathit{TC}_{1}
,
\mathit{TC}_{2}
, and
\mathit{TC}_{3}

by

\begin{array}[]{rcl}H^{\prime}_{n}(x,y)&=&H_{n}(x,y)\wedge\mathit{RD}(x,y);\\
V^{\prime}_{n}(x,y)&=&V_{n}(x,y)\wedge\mathit{UD}(x,y);\\
\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{1}&=&\forall x\exists y\,H^{\prime}_{n}(x,y);\\
\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{2}&=&\forall x\exists y\,V^{\prime}_{n}(x,y);\\
\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{3}&=&\forall x\forall y\,(\exists z\,(H^{\prime}_{n}(x,z)\wedge V^{\prime}_{n}(z,y))\leftrightarrow\exists z\,(V^{\prime}_{n}(x,z)\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(z,y)).\\
\end{array}

Using the formulas defined above, we can redefine the formula
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}
describing the special
T_{n}
-tiling. Let

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathit{Tiling}^{\prime}_{n}&=&\mathit{TC}_{0}\wedge\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{1}\wedge\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{2}\wedge\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{3}\wedge\mathit{TC}_{4}\wedge\mathit{DSR}\wedge\mathit{DSU}.\end{array}

We may think of
\mathit{Tiling}^{\prime}_{n}
as a formula describing two independent tilings: one of them is the special
T_{n}
-tiling, another is a “hidden” tiling generated by sixteen tile types each of which is defined by a pair
(R_{i},U_{j})
, where
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, see Figure
12
(the edges of the tiles are marked by four colors instead of words).

Finally, we define a modification of
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}&=&\mathit{Tiling}^{\prime}_{n}\to\exists x\,P_{1}(x).\\
\end{array}

Lemma 3.8

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QCl}
.

Just follow, step by step, to the proof of Lemma
3.1
.

Corollary 3.9

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QCl}\uplus\bm{sib}
.

3.2
indicating significant changes.

n\in\mathbb{Y}
,

, for some

, and
r=\max\{m,n+1\}
.

Figure 13:
Replacement of cycles

We define a model similar to the model in the proof of Lemma
3.2
but with cycles of length
4
instead of length
2
(see Figure
13
for “horizontal” cycles). Let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}D&=&\{0,\ldots,r+4\}\times\{0,\ldots,r+4\}.\\
\end{array}

Define a model
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{M}\models P(\langle i,j\rangle,\langle i^{\prime},j^{\prime}\rangle)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{either $|i^{\prime}-i|=1$ and $j^{\prime}=j$,}\\
&&\mbox{or $i^{\prime}=i$ and $|j^{\prime}-j|=1$,}\\
&&\mbox{or $\{i^{\prime},i\}=\{r+1,r+4\}$ and $j^{\prime}=j$,}\\
&&\mbox{or $i^{\prime}=i$ and $\{j^{\prime},j\}=\{r+1,r+4\}$;}\\
\bm{M}\models P_{k}(\langle i,j\rangle)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&f_{n}(i,j)=t_{k}^{n};\\
\bm{M}\models R_{k}(\langle i,j\rangle)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&|i-k+1|\mathrel{\vdots}4;\\
\bm{M}\models U_{k}(\langle i,j\rangle)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&|j-k+1|\mathrel{\vdots}4,\end{array}

see Figure
14
.

Then, by the definition of
\bm{M}
, we obtain
\bm{M}\models\mathit{Tiling}^{\prime}_{n}
. Since
n\not\in\mathbb{X}
, there is no
i,j\in\mathds{N}
such that
f_{n}(i,j)=t_{1}
, therefore,
\bm{M}\not\models\exists x\,P_{1}(x)
.

Notice that
\bm{M}\models\bm{sib}
.
Hence,
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}\uplus\bm{sib}
.

Remark 3.11

Observe that the length of every
P
-cycle in the model
\bm{M}
defined in the proof of Lemma
3.10
is even.

Corollary 3.12

If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

Remark 3.13

Theorem
3.3
follows from Lemma
3.8
and Corollary
3.12
.

Corollary 3.14

If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
(\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}^{\phantom{i}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}\uplus\bm{sib}
.

It is sufficient to expand the model
\bm{M}
defined in the proof of Lemma
3.10
so that the formula
\forall x\,G(x)
becomes true.

To simulate unary predicate letters
P_{m}
,
R_{i}
, and
U_{j}
, we define formulas similar to
\mathit{tile}_{k}(x)
.
Let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\pi_{0}(x)&=&G(x);\\
\pi_{1}^{y}(x)&=&\exists y\,(P(x,y)\wedge G(y));\\
\pi_{k+1}^{y}(x)&=&\exists y\,(P(x,y)\wedge\pi^{x}_{k}(y)),\\
\end{array}

where
k\in\mathds{N}^{+}
. Informally,
\pi_{k}^{y}(x)
says that there is a
P
-path of length
k
from
x
to some element of a grid. For every
k\in\mathds{N}^{++}
, define

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{path}_{k}^{y}(x)&=&\pi_{k}^{y}(x)\wedge\neg\pi_{k-1}^{y}(x)\wedge\neg\pi_{k-2}^{y}(x)\wedge\forall y\,(P(x,y)\to\pi_{k-1}^{x}(y)).\end{array}

Formula
\mathit{path}_{k}(x)
says that the length of a shortest
P
-path from
x
to an element of a grid is
k
and, moreover, every element
P
-adjacent to
x
is closer to the grid than
x
. Next, let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\sigma^{y}_{k,0}(x)&=&\mathit{path}_{k}^{y}(x);\\
\sigma_{k,i+1}^{y}(x)&=&\exists y\,(P(x,y)\wedge\sigma_{k,i}^{x}(y)),\\
\end{array}

where
k\in\mathds{N}^{++}
and
i\in\mathds{N}
. The formula
\sigma^{y}_{k,i}(x)
says that there is a
P
-path of length
i
from
x
to an element such that a shortest path from it to a grid is of length
k
and every element
P
-adjacent to it is closer to the grid. Define

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k}(x)&=&\sigma_{k+2,k+2}^{y}(x);\\
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k}(y)&=&\sigma_{k+2,k+2}^{x}(y);\\
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k}(z)&=&\sigma_{k+2,k+2}^{x}(z),\end{array}

where
k\in\mathds{N}
.

Remark 3.15

Formulas
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k}(x)
and
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k}(y)
contain no variables except
x
and
y
; also they contain no predicate letters except
P
and
G
.

Now, let us define formulas simulating
G(u)
with
u\in\{x,y,z\}
; the formulas are similar to
\mathit{grid}(u)
but slightly more complicated and contain three variables:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathit{triangle}^{yz}(x)&=&\exists y\exists z\,(P(x,y)\wedge P(y,z)\wedge P(z,x));\\
\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(x)&=&\neg\mathit{triangle}^{yz}(x)\wedge\exists y\,(P(x,y)\wedge\mathit{triangle}^{xz}(y));\\
\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(y)&=&\neg\mathit{triangle}^{xz}(y)\wedge\exists y\,(P(y,x)\wedge\mathit{triangle}^{yz}(x));\\
\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(z)&=&\neg\mathit{triangle}^{xy}(z)\wedge\exists y\,(P(z,x)\wedge\mathit{triangle}^{yz}(x)).\end{array}

Let us define a function
S_{1}
, similar to
S_{0}
, associating with each formula
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
a formula containing no predicate letters except
P
. Define
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
as the formula obtained from
\exists x\,G(x)\to(\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}^{\phantom{i}}
by replacing

•

each occurrence of
P_{m}(x)
,
P_{m}(y)
, and
P_{m}(z)
with
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{m}(x)
,
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{m}(y)
, and
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{m}(z)
, respectively, where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
;

•

then each occurrence of
R_{i}(x)
,
R_{i}(y)
,
R_{i}(z)
with
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+i}(x)
,
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+i}(y)
,
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+i}(z)
, respectively, where
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
;

•

then each occurrence of
U_{j}(x)
,
U_{j}(y)
,
U_{j}(z)
with
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(x)
,
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(y)
,
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(z)
, respectively, where
1\leqslant j\leqslant 4
;

•

and then each occurrence of
G(x)
,
G(y)
, and
G(z)
with

\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(x)
,
\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(y)
, and
\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(z)
, respectively.

Notice that these replacements are formula substitutions.
4
4

4

See footnote
3
.

Lemma 3.16

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QCl}
.

Follows from Lemmas
3.8
and
3.4
taking into account that
\mathbf{QCl}
is closed by Substitution.

Lemma 3.17

If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Y}
. Then
\bm{M}\not\models\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
, where
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
is the model defined in the proof of Lemma
3.10
. Without a loss of generality, we may assume that
\bm{M}\models\forall x\,G(x)
, see the proof of Corollary
3.14
. Then,

\begin{array}[]{c}\bm{M}\penalty\ \not\models\penalty\ \exists x\,G(x)\to(\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}^{\phantom{i}}.\end{array}

We extend
\bm{M}
to a finite model refuting
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
.

a
e_{m}^{a}
e_{k_{n}+i}^{a}
e_{k_{n}+j+4}^{a}
e_{k_{n}+9}^{a}
e_{k_{n}+10}^{a}
e_{P}^{a}
e_{R}^{a}
e_{U}^{a}
e_{0}^{a}
e_{2}^{a}
e_{1}^{a}

Figure 15:
Simulation of
P_{m}(a)
,
R_{i}(a)
,
U_{j}(a)
, and
G(a)

For every individual
a\in\mathcal{D}
, let us add new elements

c_{0}^{a},c_{1}^{a},c_{2}^{a}
;

e_{0}^{a},\ldots,e_{k_{n}+10}^{a}
;

e_{P}^{a},e_{R}^{a},e_{U}^{a}

to
\mathcal{D}
,
expand
\mathcal{I}(P)
with

•

\langle a,c_{0}^{a}\rangle,\langle c_{0}^{a},c_{1}^{a}\rangle,\langle c_{1}^{a},c_{2}^{a}\rangle,\langle c_{2}^{a},c_{0}^{a}\rangle
;

•

\langle a,e_{0}^{a}\rangle,\langle e_{0}^{a},e_{1}^{a}\rangle,\ldots,\langle e_{k_{n}+9}^{a},e_{k_{n}+10}^{a}\rangle
;

•

\langle e_{m}^{a},e_{P}^{a}\rangle
if
\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a)
with
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
;

•

\langle e_{k_{n}+i}^{a},e_{R}^{a}\rangle
if
\bm{M}\models R_{i}(a)
with
i\in\{1,2,3,4\}
;

•

\langle e_{k_{n}+j+4}^{a},e_{U}^{a}\rangle
if
\bm{M}\models U_{j}(a)
with
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
,

and then take the symmetric closure of the resulting relation.
Let
\bm{M}^{\prime}=\langle\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\mathcal{I}^{\prime}\rangle
be the resulting model; see Figure
15
.

By the definition of
\bm{M}^{\prime}
, for every
a\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$a\in\mathcal{D}$.}\\
\end{array}

Indeed, if
a\in\mathcal{D}
, then
\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(a)
since

\bm{M}^{\prime}\penalty\ \models\penalty\ P(a,c_{0}^{a})\wedge P(c_{0}^{a},c_{1}^{a})\wedge P(c_{1}^{a},c_{2}^{a})\wedge P(c_{2}^{a},c_{0}^{a}).

For the converse implication, assume that
\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(a)
. Then there is an element
c\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
such that
\bm{M}^{\prime}\models P(a,c)
and
c
is in a cycle of length
3
. But the length of every
P
-cycle in
\mathcal{D}
is even (see Remark
3.11
), therefore,
c\in\{c_{0}^{b},c_{1}^{b},c_{2}^{b}\}
, for some
b\in\mathcal{D}
. Observe that
\bm{M}^{\prime}\not\models\mathit{grid}^{\prime}(c_{i}^{b})
, for every
i\in\{0,1,2\}
, and hence,
a\not\in\{c_{0}^{b},c_{1}^{b},c_{2}^{b}\}
. Then, by the construction of
\bm{M}^{\prime}
, we conclude that
a=b
and
c=c_{0}^{b}
, so, in particular,
a\in\mathcal{D}
.

Also, it is not hard to check that, by the definition of
\bm{M}^{\prime}
, for every
i\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
,
j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, and
a\in\mathcal{D}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{M}\models\hfill P_{i}\hfill(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{i}(a);\\
\bm{M}\models\hfill R_{j}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j}(a);\\
\bm{M}\models\hfill U_{j}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\bm{M}^{\prime}\models\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(a).\end{array}

Using these, we obtain that
\bm{M}^{\prime}\not\models S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
. Notice that the model
\bm{M}^{\prime}
is finite.
Thus,
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

As a result, we obtain an analogue of the Trakhtenbrot theorem for the theory of symmetric irreflexive binary relation when the language contains only three variables (and a single binary predicate letter).

Theorem 3.18

Theories
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Immediate from Lemmas
3.16
and
3.17
.

This theorem provides us with an answer to the question raised by S. Speranski: is
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
decidable in the language with three variables? As we can see, it is undecidable; moreover, it is not recursively enumerable (since
\mathbf{QCl}
is recursively enumerable) and even
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-complete (we will make a general remark on this below, see Proposition
3.26
and Corollary
3.28
).

Corollary 3.19

Theories
\mathbf{SIB}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

If
\mathbf{SIB}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively separable, then
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
should be recursively separable, since
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
, which contradicts Theorem
3.18
.

Now, let us turn to
\mathbf{SRB}
and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
. Observe that a binary relation is irreflexive and symmetric if, and only if, its complement is reflexive and symmetric.
For a formula
\varphi
in the language containing a binary predicate letter
P
, define
{\sim}\varphi
as the formula obtained from
\varphi
by substituting
\neg P(x_{1},x_{2})
instead of
P(x_{1},x_{2})
, i.e., by placing
\neg
before every occurrence of
P
in
\varphi
. Then, clearly,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi\in\mathbf{SIB}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&{\sim}\varphi\in\mathbf{SRB};\\
\varphi\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&{\sim}\varphi\in\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}},\\
\end{array}

This observation yields us to the following corollaries.

Corollary 3.20

Theories
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Corollary 3.21

Theories
\mathbf{SRB}
and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

We can generalize these statements to a lot of other theories.

Theorem 3.22

Let
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
be theories of a binary predicate such that
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
and also
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
or
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}
. Then
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language with three variables.

Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively separable in such a language. If
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
, then
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
should be recursively separable; if
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}
, then
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
should be recursively separable. In any case, we obtain a contradiction.

3.5
Remarks on complexity

We make remarks on the complexity of the theories
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\mathit{fin}}
in the statement of Theorem
3.22
. It is not difficult to show that
\Gamma
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\mathit{fin}}
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard. We explain this in terms of the constructions described above. To this end, consider, instead of
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
a
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete set
\mathbb{A}
of natural numbers and the empty set which we denote
\mathbb{B}
. Notice that these sets, unlike
\mathbb{X}
and
\mathbb{Y}
, are recursively separable. We are interested in the function
f_{\mathbb{AB}}\colon\mathds{N}\to\mathds{N}
defined by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}f_{\mathbb{AB}}(x)&=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}0&\mbox{if $x\in\mathbb{A}$;}\\
1&\mbox{if $x\in\mathbb{B}$;}\\
\mbox{undefined}&\mbox{if $x\not\in\mathbb{A}\cup\mathbb{B}$.}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{array}

This function is computable since
\mathbb{A}
is recursively enumerable and, taking into account that
\mathbb{B}
is empty,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}f_{\mathbb{AB}}(x)&=&\left\{\begin{array}[]{rl}0&\mbox{if $x\in\mathbb{A}$;}\\
\mbox{undefined}&\mbox{if $x\not\in\mathbb{A}.\phantom{{}\cup\mathbb{B}}$}\\
\end{array}\right.\end{array}

So, it is computable by a Turing machine

M_{1}=(\Sigma_{1},Q_{1},q_{0},F_{1},\delta_{1})
with halting states
q_{\mathbb{A}},q_{\mathbb{B}}\in F_{1}
such that

•

if
m\in\mathbb{A}
, then
q_{\mathbb{A}}!M_{1}(\overline{m})
;

•

if
m\in\mathbb{B}
, then
q_{\mathbb{B}}!M_{1}(\overline{m})
;

•

if
m\not\in\mathbb{A}\cup\mathbb{B}
, then
\neg!M_{1}(\overline{m})
;

notice that, since
\mathbb{B}
is empty,
q_{\mathbb{B}}!M_{1}(\overline{m})
never heppens and
m\not\in\mathbb{A}\cup\mathbb{B}
means that
m\not\in\mathbb{A}
.
Then, we can repeat the construction with tiles for
M_{1}
and define, for every
n\in\mathds{N}
, a set
T^{\prime}_{n}
of tile types and a special
T^{\prime}_{n}
-tiling described by formulas
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}
and
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}
so that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n\in\mathbb{A}&\Longrightarrow&S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}};\\
n\in\mathbb{A}&\Longrightarrow&S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}};\\
\end{array}

(3.1)

and

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n\in\mathbb{A}&\Longrightarrow&{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}};\\
n\in\mathbb{A}&\Longrightarrow&{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\not\in\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}};\\
\end{array}

(3.2)

we leave the details to the reader.

Observe that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n\not\in\mathbb{A}&\Longrightarrow&\phantom{{\sim}}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}\not\in\mathbf{SIB};\\
n\not\in\mathbb{A}&\Longrightarrow&{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}\not\in\mathbf{SRB}.\\
\end{array}

(3.3)

Indeed, if
n\not\in\mathbb{A}
, then
\neg!M_{1}(\overline{n})
, and the special
T^{\prime}_{n}
-tiling does not contain a tile of a type
t
with
\mathop{\hbox to5.52pt{\vbox to5.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 7.09 L 7.09 7.09 L 7.09 0 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} {}\lxSVG@fillstroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.09 L 3.54 3.54 L 7.09 7.09}{} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 7.09 L 3.54 3.54 L 7.09 7.09}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
{}{{}}{}
{}{}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.09 7.09 L 0 7.09}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}t=\#q_{\mathbb{A}}
, and we can use the tiling to obtain a required countermodels for
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}
and for
{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}
.

Then, using the first lines of
(\ref{eq:ast:1})
and
(\ref{eq:ast:2})
, we obtain that, for every theory
\Gamma
such that
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n\in\mathbb{A}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}\in\Gamma\end{array}

and, using the first line of
(\ref{eq:ast:2})
and the second line of
(\ref{eq:ast:3})
, we obtain that, for every theory
\Gamma
such that
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n\in\mathbb{A}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{A}}\in\Gamma.\end{array}

These observations yield the following proposition.

Proposition 3.23

If
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\Gamma
and also
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
or
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}
, then
\Gamma
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard in a language with a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Remark 3.24

If, additionally,
\Gamma
is recursively enumerable, then
\Gamma
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete.

Corollary 3.25

Theories
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
,
\mathbf{SIB}
, and
\mathbf{SRB}
are
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete in a language with a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

To prove
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hardness of
\Gamma^{\prime}
, we proceed in a similar way.

Before proceeding, note that there is a minor difficulty that we need to overcome.
By the second line of (
3.1
),
n\in\mathbb{A}
implies
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
. In addition, we want
n\not\in\mathbb{A}
to imply
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
(and then
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}
). But the last implication can be wrong. Indeed, imagine that
M_{1}
, starting with input
\overline{n}
, turns out to be in the same configuration twice (and hence, we obtain a computation with an infinite cycle). Then, as we have seen above, there exists a finite model for the special
T^{\prime}_{n}
-tiling, and it refutes
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}
. To avoid this, we have to “remove” all cycles in computations of
M_{1}
(except artificial ones involving
q_{\mathbb{A}}
). Fortunately, this is possible.
To show this, consider a Turing machine
M
executing
M_{1}
step by step and saving all configurations of the computation of
M_{1}
(with the content of the tape from the leftmost cell to the last non-empty cell or the scanned) into a sequence. For every new configuration of
M_{1}
, machine
M
compares it with every one of the sequence; if two configurations are equal and their state components are different from
q_{\mathbb{A}}
, then
M
stops the computation of
M_{1}
and then starts moving the head to the right on each step. If
M
sees a new configuration with
q_{\mathbb{A}}
, then
M
deletes the sequence and then executes
M_{1}
on the input (without any controlling actions). Then, clearly,
M
computes the same function as
M_{1}
and its computations do not contain cycles (except involving
q_{\mathbb{A}}
). So, without a loss of generality we may assume that
M_{1}
already satisfies this property.

In this case, it is not hard to see that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n\not\in\mathbb{A}&\Longrightarrow&\phantom{{\sim}}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}};\\
n\not\in\mathbb{A}&\Longrightarrow&{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}.\\
\end{array}

(3.4)

Indeed, let
n\not\in\mathbb{A}
. Then
\neg!M_{1}(\overline{n})
, therefore, the computation of
M_{1}
on the input
\overline{n}
is infinite and acyclic. This means that every model validating the formula describing the special
T^{\prime}_{n}
-tiling, i.e., the premise of
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}
, is infinite. Thus, the premise of formula
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}
is refuted in every finite model; therefore,
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}
is true in every finite model. Then,
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}
. Hence,
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}
and
{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

Using
(\ref{eq:ast:4})
and the second lines of
(\ref{eq:ast:1})
and
(\ref{eq:ast:2})
, we obtain that, for every theory
\Gamma
such that
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n\not\in\mathbb{A}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\Gamma\\
\end{array}

and, for every theory
\Gamma
such that
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}n\not\in\mathbb{A}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{B}}\in\Gamma.\\
\end{array}

These observations lead to another proposition.

Proposition 3.26

If
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\Gamma
and also
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
or
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}
, then
\Gamma_{\mathit{fin}}
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard in a language with a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Remark 3.27

If, additionally, the class of finite
\Gamma
-models is recursively enumerable, then
\Gamma_{\mathit{fin}}
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-complete.

Corollary 3.28

Theories
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}
,
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
, and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-complete in a language with a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

3.6
Positive formulas

All the results above can be obtained using positive formulas only; we make a short remark on this (in fact, we will need positive formulas only below when dealing with superintuitionistic logics).
We call an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
positive
if it does not contain occurrences of
\bot
. Note that positive formulas do not contain occurrences of
\neg
as well, since it is an abbreviation defined with the use of
\bot
.

We just simulate
\bot
by the formula

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathit{false}&=&\forall x\forall y\,P(x,y).\end{array}

For an
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi
, let
\varphi^{+}
be the formula obtained from
\varphi
by replacing every occurrence of
\bot
with
\mathit{false}
.

Lemma 3.29

Let
\bm{M}\not\models\mathit{false}
. Then, for every formula
\varphi
,

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\bm{M}\models\varphi&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\bm{M}\models\varphi^{+}.\end{array}

Obvious since
\bm{M}\models\mathit{false}\leftrightarrow\bot
.

Corollary 3.30

Let
\bm{M}\not\models S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
. Then,
\bm{M}\not\models(S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})^{+}
.

Observe that
\bm{M}
satisfies the condition of Lemma
3.29
.

Corollary 3.31

Let
\bm{M}\not\models{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
. Then,
\bm{M}\not\models({\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})^{+}
.

The same.

Corollary 3.32

Let
\bm{M}
be a model and
\varphi
be a formula containing no predicate letters except
P
such that
\bm{M}\not\models\varphi^{+}
. Then
\bm{M}\not\models\varphi
.

Observe that
\bm{M}
satisfies the condition of Lemma
3.29
, since otherwise every positive formula containing
P
as the unique predicate letter should be true in
\bm{M}
. Then, by Lemma
3.29
,
\bm{M}\not\models\varphi
.

Corollaries
3.30
–
3.32
ensure that we can replace
S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
and
{\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
with
(S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})^{+}
and
({\sim}S_{1}\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})^{+}
, respectively, in all constructions above. Let us formulate statements immediately following from this observation.

Theorem 3.33

Let
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}
be theories of a binary predicate such that
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\Gamma\subseteq\Gamma^{\prime}
and also
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
or
\Gamma^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}
. Then the positive fragments of
\Gamma
and
\Gamma^{\prime}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language with three variables.

Proposition 3.34

If
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\Gamma
and also
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
or
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}
, then the positive fragment of
\Gamma
is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard in a language with a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Corollary 3.35

The positive fragments of
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
,
\mathbf{SIB}
, and
\mathbf{SRB}
are
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete in a language with a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Proposition 3.36

If
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}\subseteq\Gamma
and also
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SIB}
or
\Gamma\subseteq\mathbf{SRB}
, then the positive fragment of
\Gamma_{\mathit{fin}}
is
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard in a language with a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Corollary 3.37

The positive fragments of
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}_{\mathit{fin}}
,
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
, and
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-complete in a language with a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

4
