Modal predicate logics

4.1
Syntax and semantics

The modal predicate language
\mathcal{ML}
extends
\mathcal{L}
by adding a modality
\Box
(“necessity”) allowing, for a formula
\varphi
, to construct the formula
\Box\varphi
. Formulas in
\mathcal{ML}
are called
modal predicate formulas
, or
\mathcal{ML}
-formulas
. Define
\Diamond
(“possibility”) by
\Diamond\varphi=\neg\Box\neg\varphi
.
From now on, we identify the language
\mathcal{ML}
with the set of
\mathcal{ML}
-formulas.

We call a set
L
of
\mathcal{ML}
-formulas
modal predicate logic
if
L
is closed under Substitution.
5
5

5

For predicate substitution see
[
12
]
.
In fact, below we shall consider only modal predicate logics of some special classes; in particular, they contain
\mathbf{QCl}
and are closed, additionally, under Modus Ponens and Generalization.

To study modal predicate logics we shall use Kripke semantics. Here, we describe the expanding domains semantics only; it is enough for a wide class of logics
[
12
]
.

A
Kripke frame
is a pair
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
, where
W
is a non-empty set of
possible worlds
and
R
is a binary
accessibility relation
on
W
. Speaking of Kripke frames, we use the standard notation
R(w)=\{w^{\prime}\in W:wRw^{\prime}\}
, so
w^{\prime}\in R(w)
means the same as
wRw^{\prime}
. We say that
\mathfrak{F}
is finite if
W
is finite; we say that
\mathfrak{F}
is reflexive, symmetric, anti-symmetric, transitive, serial, etc. if
R
is.

A
Kripke frame with domains
, or, for short, an
augmented frame
, is a pair
\bm{\mathfrak{F}}=\langle\mathfrak{F},D\rangle
, where
\mathfrak{F}
is a Kripke frame and
D
is a
domain function

D\colon W\to\mathscr{P}(\mathcal{D})
associating with every world
w\in W
a non-empty subset of a non-empty set
\mathcal{D}
of
individuals
. The set
D(w)
, also denoted by
D_{w}
, is called
the domain of the world
w
. Sets of the form
D_{w}
are also called
local domains
of
\bm{\mathfrak{F}}
and
\mathcal{D}
is called
the global domain
of
\bm{\mathfrak{F}}
; we assume that

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathcal{D}&=&\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{w\in W}}D_{w}.\end{array}

The augmented frame
\bm{\mathfrak{F}}=\langle\mathfrak{F},D\rangle
is also denoted by
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
. We say that
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
is
based on
\mathfrak{F}
.
The global domain of
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
is denoted by
D^{+}

[
12
]
.

We say that an augmented frame
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
based on a Kripke frame
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
satisfies the
expanding domain condition
if, for all
u,w\in W
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}uRw&\Longrightarrow&D_{u}\subseteq D_{w};\end{array}

[formula]

then we call
\mathfrak{F}_{D}

augmented frame with expanding domains
or, for short,
e-augmented frame
. We say that
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
satisfies the
locally constant domain condition
if, for all
u,w\in W
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}uRw&\Longrightarrow&D_{u}=D_{w},\end{array}

[formula]

and the
globally constant domain condition
if, for all
u,w\in W
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}D_{u}=D_{w}.\end{array}

[formula]

For our purposes,
{(\mathit{LCD})}
is sufficient; nevertheless, the frames satisfying
{(\mathit{LCD})}
we shall consider below, also satisfy
{(\mathit{GCD})}
. If
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
satisfies
{(\mathit{LCD})}
, then we call it
augmented frame with constant domains
or, for short,
c-augmented frame
. If
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
satisfies
{(\mathit{GCD})}
and
\mathcal{D}
is the global domain of
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
, then, following
[
12
]
, we also denote it by
\mathfrak{F}\odot\mathcal{D}
. In general, when we do not claim the domain function to satisfy any additional conditions, we obtain the class of all augmented frames; they are known as
augmented frames with varying domains
, and, to emphasize this, let us also call them
v-augmented frames
.
We will focus on e-augmented and c-augmented frames only.
6
6

6

For more background on the varying domains semantics, we refer the reader to
[
11
]
.
For convenience, sometimes we write
\langle W,R,D\rangle
for
\langle\mathfrak{F},D\rangle
with
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
.

In this section, we assume that all augmented frames satisfy
(\mathit{ED})
.

A
predicate Kripke model with expanding domains
, or simply a
Kripke model
, is a tuple
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{D},I\rangle
, where
\mathfrak{F}_{D}=\langle W,R,D\rangle
is an e-augmented frame and
I
is a map, called an
interpretation of predicate letters
, assigning to a world
w\in W
and an
n
-ary predicate letter
P
an
n
-ary relation
I(w,P)
on
D_{w}
; we also write
P^{I,w}
for
I(w,P)
and
\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
for
\langle\mathfrak{F}_{D},I\rangle
. We note that, if a predicate letter
P
is nullary (i.e.,
P
is a proposition letter), then
P^{I,w}\subseteq D^{0}_{w}=\{\langle\rangle\}
, for every
w\in W
. Conceptually,
P^{I,w}=\varnothing
corresponds to assigning the truth value “false”, and
P^{I,w}=\{\langle\rangle\}
the truth value “true”, to
P
at
w
. For a Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{D},I\rangle
, we say that
\mathfrak{M}
is
based on
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
and is
based on
\mathfrak{F}
.

An
assignment
in a model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
is a map
g
associating with every variable
x
an element
g(x)
of the global domain of the augmented frame
\langle W,R,D\rangle
. If
g
and
h
are assignments such that
g(y)=h(y)
whenever
y\neq x
, we write
g\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{=}}h
.

The truth of an
\mathcal{ML}
-formula
\varphi
at a world
w
of a model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
under an assignment
g
is defined recursively:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}P(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox{195.1634pt}{$\langle g(x_{1}),\ldots,g(x_{n})\rangle\in P^{I,w}$,}\\
\end{array}

where
P
is an
n
-ary predicate letter;

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{64.74725pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\not\models^{g}\bot;$}}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{64.74725pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}\wedge\varphi^{\prime\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{118.49464pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}$ and $\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime\prime}$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{64.74725pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}\vee\varphi^{\prime\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{118.49464pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}$\hfill or\hfill$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime\prime}$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{64.74725pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}\to\varphi^{\prime\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{118.49464pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\not\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}$\hfill or\hfill$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime\prime}$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{64.74725pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\Box\varphi^{\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\mbox{$\mathfrak{M},\parbox{7.42825pt}{$v$}\models^{g}\varphi^{\prime}$, for every $v\in R(w)$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{64.74725pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\forall x\,\varphi^{\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\mbox{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{h}\varphi^{\prime}$, for every assignment $h$ such that}\\
&&\mbox{\phantom{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g^{\prime}}\varphi^{\prime}$, }$h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{=}}g$ and $h(x)\in D_{w}$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill\parbox{64.74725pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\exists x\,\varphi^{\prime}$}}&\leftrightharpoons&\mbox{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{h}\varphi^{\prime}$, for some assignment $h$ such that}\\
&&\mbox{\phantom{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g^{\prime}}\varphi^{\prime}$, }$h\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x}}{{=}}g$ and $h(x)\in D_{w}$.}\end{array}

Let
\mathfrak{M}
,
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
,
\mathfrak{F}
, and
\mathscr{C}
be a Kripke model, an e-augmented frame, a Kripke frame, and a class of e-augmented frames, respectively,
w
a world of
\mathfrak{M}
, and
\varphi
a formula with free variables
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
; then define

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill$\mathfrak{M},w\models\varphi$}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{195.1634pt}{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi$, for every assignment $g$ such that}\\
&&\mbox{\phantom{$\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi$, }$g(x_{1}),\ldots,g(x_{n})\in D_{w}$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill$\mathfrak{M}\models\varphi$}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{195.1634pt}{$\mathfrak{M},\parbox{7.42825pt}{$v$}\models^{\phantom{g}}\varphi$, for every world $v$ of $\mathfrak{M}$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill$\mathfrak{F}_{D}\models\varphi$}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{195.1634pt}{$\parbox{13.78157pt}{$\mathfrak{M}$}\models\varphi$, for every $\mathfrak{M}$ based on $\mathfrak{F}_{D}$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill$\mathfrak{F}\models\varphi$}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{195.1634pt}{$\parbox{13.78157pt}{$\mathfrak{M}$}\models\varphi$, for every $\mathfrak{M}$ based on $\mathfrak{F}$;}\\
\parbox{87.63855pt}{{}\hfill$\mathscr{C}\models\varphi$}&\leftrightharpoons&\parbox[t]{195.1634pt}{$\parbox{13.78157pt}{$\mathfrak{F}_{D}$}\models\varphi$, for every $\mathfrak{F}_{D}\in\mathscr{C}$.}\\
\end{array}

If
\mathfrak{S}\models\varphi
, for a structure
\mathfrak{S}
(a world, a model, a frame, etc.), we say that the formula
\varphi
is
true
, or
valid
,
7
7

7

We use “valid” only for frames and classes of frames.
in (on, at)
\mathfrak{S}
; otherwise,
\varphi
is
refuted
in (on, at)
\mathfrak{S}
.
These notions, and the corresponding notations, can be extended to sets of formulas in a natural way: for a set of formulas
X
, define
\mathfrak{S}\models X
as
\mathfrak{S}\models\varphi
, for every
\varphi\in X
.

Observe that, given a Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
and
w\in W
, we can define the interpretation
I_{w}
by
I_{w}(P)=I(w,P)
, for every predicate letter
P
. Then, the tuple
\mathfrak{M}_{w}=\langle D_{w},I_{w}\rangle
is a classical model. So, we can see on the Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}
as the set
\{\mathfrak{M}_{w}:w\in W\}
of classical models structured by
R
.

Let
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
be a Kripke model,
w\in W
, and
a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\in D_{w}
; let also
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
be a formula whose free variables are among
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
. We write
\mathfrak{M},w\models\varphi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
if
\mathfrak{M},w\models^{g}\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
, where
g(x_{1})=a_{1},\ldots,g(x_{n})=a_{n}
. This notation is unambiguous since the languages we consider lack constants and the truth value of
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
does not depend on the values of variables different from
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
.
Given a non-atomic formula
\varphi(x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})
with
x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}
being the list of its free variables,
8
8

8

We assume that the variables in the list are in a certain order.
define, for every
w\in W
, the
n
-ary relation
\varphi^{I,w}
:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi^{I,w}&=&\{(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in D_{w}^{n}:\mathfrak{M},w\models\varphi(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\}.\end{array}

In fact, this definition expands
I
to the set of all formulas. We write
\varphi^{I,w}(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})
rather than
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{n})\in\varphi^{I,w}
.

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a class of e-augmented frames. Define the
modal predicate logic
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}}\mathscr{C}
of the class
\mathscr{C}
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}}\mathscr{C}&=&\{\varphi\in\mathcal{ML}:\mathscr{C}\models\varphi\}.\end{array}

Let
\mathscr{C}
be a class of Kripke frames and
\alpha\in\{\mathit{c},\mathit{e}\}
; define

•

\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathscr{C}
be the class of finite Kripke frames of
\mathscr{C}
;

•

\mathop{\mbox{{{aug}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{all}}}{\mathscr{C}}
be the classes of
\alpha
-augmented frames based on Kripke frames of
\mathscr{C}
;

•

\mathop{\mbox{{{aug}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}{\mathscr{C}}
be the classes of
\alpha
-augmented frames based on Kripke frames of
\mathscr{C}
, whose local domains are finite;

•

\mathop{\mbox{{{aug}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}{\mathscr{C}}
be the classes of
\alpha
-augmented frames based on frames of
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathscr{C}
.

In addition, let
\beta\in\{\mathit{all},\mathit{dfin},\mathit{wfin}\}
. Define the logic
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\beta}}}\mathscr{C}
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\beta}}}\mathscr{C}&=&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{aug}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\beta}}}\mathscr{C}.\end{array}

For a Kripke frame
\mathfrak{F}
, we write
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\beta}}}\mathfrak{F}
rather than
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\beta}}}\{\mathfrak{F}\}
.
Notice that

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{\beta}}}\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{\beta}}}\mathscr{C},&&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\mathit{all}}}}\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\mathit{dfin}}}}\mathscr{C},&&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\mathit{all}}}}\mathscr{C}\subseteq\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\alpha}}_{\mathit{\mathit{wfin}}}}\mathscr{C}.\end{array}

For a modal predicate logic
L
, denote by
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
the class of Kripke frames validating
L
. Define

\begin{array}[]{lcl}L_{\mathit{dfin}}&=&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L;\\
L_{\mathit{wfin}}&=&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L.\\
\end{array}

For sets
X
and
Y
of
\mathcal{ML}
-formulas, define

•

X\oplus Y
to be the smallest modal predicate logic, containing
X\cup Y
and closed under Substitution, Modus Ponens, Generalization, and Necessitation;

•

X+Y
to be the smallest modal predicate logic, containing
X\cup Y
and closed under Substitution, Modus Ponens, and Generalization.

We write
X\oplus\varphi
and
X+\varphi
rather than
X\oplus\{\varphi\}
and
X+\{\varphi\}
, respectively, if
\varphi
is an
\mathcal{ML}
-formula.

Define the Barcan formula
\bm{bf}
by
\bm{bf}=\forall x\,\Box Q(x)\to\Box\forall x\,Q(x)
, where
Q
is a unary predicate letter; it is well known and easy to check that
\bm{bf}
is valid on an e-augmented frame
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
if, and only if,
\mathfrak{F}_{D}
is a c-augmented frame.

For further definitions we need refer to propositional logics. We assume that the reader is familiar with modal propositional logics; for details, consult
[
7
]
.
Also, we assume the modal propositional language be the propositional fragment of
\mathcal{M}
, where propositional variables are proposition letters.
9
9

9

Formally, this is not the case: if
p
is a propositional variable, then
p
is a propositional formula; if
p
is a proposition letter, then
p
is not an
\mathcal{ML}
-formula but
p()
is. So, we identify
p()
with
p
.

We call
10
10

10

The claim of closure under Necessitation is sometimes replaced with the claim that Necessitation is postulated as an inference rule; this approach is more subtle but we do not need it, even without a loss of generality.
a propositional modal logic
L

normal
if it contains
\mathbf{K}
and closed under Substitution, Modes Ponens, and Necessitation; we will deal mostly with predicate counterparts of normal modal propositional logics.
For a normal modal propositional logic
L
, define
\mathbf{Q}L
and
\mathbf{Q}L\mathbf{.bf}
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{Q}L&=&L\oplus\mathbf{QCl};\\
\mathbf{Q}L{\mathbf{.bf}}&=&\mathbf{Q}L\oplus\bm{bf}.\end{array}

For convenience, we recall definitions for some special modal predicate logics we will refer to:

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\mathbf{QK}&=&\mathbf{QCl}&\!\!\oplus&\Box(p\to q)\to(\Box p\to\Box q);\\
\mathbf{QT}&=&\mathbf{QK}&\!\!\oplus&\Box p\to p;\\
\mathbf{QD}&=&\mathbf{QK}&\!\!\oplus&\Diamond\top;\\
\mathbf{QKB}&=&\mathbf{QK}&\!\!\oplus&p\to\Box\Diamond p;\\
\mathbf{QKTB}&=&\mathbf{QT}&\!\!\oplus&\mathbf{QKB};\\
\mathbf{QK4}&=&\mathbf{QK}&\!\!\oplus&\Box p\to\Box\Box p;\\
\mathbf{QK4B}&=&\mathbf{QK4}&\!\!\oplus&\mathbf{QKB};\\
\mathbf{QS4}&=&\mathbf{QT}&\!\!\oplus&\mathbf{QK4};\\
\mathbf{QK5}&=&\mathbf{QK}&\!\!\oplus&\Diamond\Box p\to\Box p;\\
\mathbf{QS5}&=&\mathbf{QS4}&\!\!\oplus&\mathbf{QK5};\\
\mathbf{QK45}&=&\mathbf{QK4}&\!\!\oplus&\mathbf{QK5};\\
\mathbf{QKD45}&=&\mathbf{QD}&\!\!\oplus&\mathbf{QK45};\\
\mathbf{QGL}&=&\mathbf{QK4}&\!\!\oplus&\Box(\Box p\to p)\to\Box p;\\
\mathbf{QGrz}&=&\mathbf{QS4}&\!\!\oplus&\Box(\Box(p\to\Box p)\to p)\to p;\\
\mathbf{QwGrz}&=&\mathbf{QK4}&\!\!\oplus&\Box^{+}(\Box(p\to\Box p)\to p)\to p;\\
\mathbf{QTriv}&=&\mathbf{QK}&\!\!\oplus&p\leftrightarrow\Box p;\\
\mathbf{QVer}&=&\mathbf{QK}&\!\!\oplus&\Box\bot,\\
\end{array}

where
\Box^{+}
is defined by
\Box^{+}\varphi=\varphi\wedge\Box\varphi
.
Notice that some of the logics are Kripke complete,
11
11

11

A modal predicate logic
L
is
Kripke complete
if there exists a class
\mathscr{C}
of e-augmented frames such that
L=\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}}\mathscr{C}
.
some are not
[
27
,
59
,
12
,
51
]
, some of them contain the Barcan formula (for example,
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QTriv}
,
\mathbf{QVer}
), some do not; for details see
[
12
]
.

Let us make a remark. Note that, generally speaking, it is possible that, for a modal predicate logic
L
, the class
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is empty or degenerate but the class
\mathop{\mbox{{{AF}}}}^{\mathit{e}}L
of e-augmented frames validating
L
is not empty and not degenerate. For example, Kripke frames for
\mathbf{QK.bf}
may contain only singletons and clusters not seeing each other, while the class
\mathop{\mbox{{{AF}}}}^{\mathit{e}}\mathbf{QK.bf}
consists of all c-augmented frames; something similar is true for
\mathbf{QK}\oplus\Box\bm{bf}
,
\mathbf{QK}\oplus\Box\Box\bm{bf}
, etc.; the logic
\mathbf{QK}\oplus\forall x\forall y\,(P(x)\leftrightarrow P(y))
is not validated by a Kripke frame, but is validated by every e-augmented frame whose local domains are singletons. Therefore, it seems reasonable to deal with
\mathop{\mbox{{{AF}}}}^{\mathit{e}}L
rather than
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
; in some sense it is really the case.
At the same time, the benefit of such an approach is not significant, as for the most interesting logics, replacing the class of Kripke frames with the class of e-augmented frames does not result in a different logic.
Nevertheless, we will be interested in logics of c-augmented frames; but we can describe the locally constant domain condition using the Barcan formula, and the difficulty does not arise. Thus, for such logics, the use of Kripke frames instead of e-augmented frames will not lead to a violation of generality.

4.2
Recursive inseparability of dyadic fragments

Here, we make some straightforward observations concerning recursive inseparability of modal logics when their language contains a binary predicate letter.

We start with recursive inseparability of a modal predicate logic and the logic of its e-augmented frames with finite local domains.

Lemma 4.1

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal logics such that
L\subseteq L^{\prime}
,
L\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}
, and
L^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Immediate from Theorem
3.7
.

Corollary 4.2

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal logics such that
L\subseteq L^{\prime}
,
L\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}
, and
L^{\prime}\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Also, as a corollaries, we obtain the following propositions.

Proposition 4.3

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal logics such that
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Observe that then
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L\neq\varnothing
, and therefore
L\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}
. Also, observe that
L^{\prime}_{dfin}\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
. Thus, we can apply Lemma
4.1
to
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}
.

Proposition 4.4

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal logics such that
\mathbf{QK}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and also either
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{QTriv}
or
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{QVer}
. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Observe that
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}\mathbf{QTriv}\neq\varnothing
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}\mathbf{QVer}\neq\varnothing
. Also,
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq\mathbf{QK}
. Thus, we can apply Proposition
4.3
to
L
and
L^{\prime}
.

Proposition 4.5

Let
L
be one of
\mathbf{QK}
,
\mathbf{QT}
,
\mathbf{QD}
,
\mathbf{QKB}
,
\mathbf{QKTB}
,
\mathbf{QK4}
,
\mathbf{QS4}
,
\mathbf{QK5}
,
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
,
\mathbf{QK4B}
,
\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}
. Then
L
and
L_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Observe that
\mathbf{QK}\subseteq L
and also
L\subseteq\mathbf{QTriv}
or
L\subseteq\mathbf{QVer}
; then apply Proposition
4.4
taking
L^{\prime}=L
.

Now, let us turn to the situation when a modal predicate logic and the logic of its finite Kripke frames are considered; notice that the local domains of finite frames can be infinite.

Proposition 4.6

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal logics such that
L\subseteq L^{\prime}
,
L\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}
, and
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Follows from the Church theorem
[
8
,
60
]
or Theorem
3.7
. Indeed, Theorem
3.7
gives us that
\mathbf{QCl}
is undecidable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables. Suppose that
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively separable. Then
L\cap\mathcal{L}
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}\cap\mathcal{L}
should also be recursively separable. But
L\cap\mathcal{L}=L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}
(since
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
), and then
\mathbf{QCl}
should be decidable, that is a contradiction. Thus,
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable.

Corollary 4.7

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal logics such that
L\subseteq L^{\prime}
,
L\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}
, and
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

This lemma provides us with some other corollaries.

Proposition 4.8

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal logics such that
\mathbf{QK}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and also either
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{QTriv}
or
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{QVer}
. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Observe that
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}\mathbf{QTriv}\neq\varnothing
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}\mathbf{QVer}\neq\varnothing
. Also,
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq\mathbf{QK}
. Thus, we can apply Proposition
4.6
to
L
and
L^{\prime}
.

Proposition 4.9

Let
L
be one of
\mathbf{QK}
,
\mathbf{QT}
,
\mathbf{QD}
,
\mathbf{QKB}
,
\mathbf{QKTB}
,
\mathbf{QK4}
,
\mathbf{QS4}
,
\mathbf{QK5}
,
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
,
\mathbf{QK4B}
,
\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}
. Then
L
and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a binary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Observe that
\mathbf{QK}\subseteq L
and also
L\subseteq\mathbf{QTriv}
or
L\subseteq\mathbf{QVer}
; then apply Proposition
4.8
taking
L^{\prime}=L
.

These observations show us that the questions about the recursive inseparability of logics
L
and
L_{\mathit{dfin}}
or logics
L
and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
, for a “natural” modal predicate logic
L
, is trivial due to the Trakhtenbrot theorem.

4.3
Monadic fragments and three variables

The monadic fragments of
\mathbf{QCl}
and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{wfin}}
coincide and are decidable
[
3
, Chapter 21]
. At the same time, the monadic fragments of many modal predicate logics are undecidable, even in languages with a single unary predicate letter and two-three individual variables
[
43
,
44
,
47
]
. Here, we improve many of the known results concerning languages with three individual variables.

We say that a Kripke frame
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
satisfies the
Kripke–Hughes–Cresswell condition
(for short,
KHC
) if
R(w)
is infinite for some
w\in W
. We say that a modal predicate logic
L
is
KHC-friendly
if there exists a Kripke frame
\mathfrak{F}\in\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
satisfying KHC. The monadic fragments of KHC-friendly modal predicate logics are known to be undecidable
[
24
,
20
]
. For our purposes, we need a weaker condition.
We say that a class
\mathscr{C}
of Kripke frames satisfies the
weak Kripke–Hughes–Cresswell condition
(for short,
wKHC
) if, for every
n\in\mathds{N}
, there exists a Kripke frame
\langle W,R\rangle\in\mathscr{C}
with
w\in W
such that
|R(w)|\geqslant n
. Clearly, if a class of Kripke frames contains a frame satisfying KHC, then it also satisfies wKHC. However, if
\mathscr{C}
contains only finite Kripke frames, then it can not satisfy KHC; at the same time, the class of all finite Kripke frames satisfies wKHC.
Neither Kripke
[
24
]
nor Hughes and Cresswell
[
20
]
consider modal predicate logics determined by classes of finite Kripke frames.
Skvortsov investigated such logics in the context of superintuitionistic predicate logics
[
55
]
; later, the algorithmic properties of monadic fragments of modal and superintuitionistic predicate logics, determined by classes of finite Kripke frames, were studied
[
44
,
47
]
.
We say that a class
\mathscr{C}
of Kripke frames is a
Skvortsov class
if the class
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathscr{C}
satisfies wKHC.

Let
Q
be a unary predicate letter and
P
a binary predicate letter. Let
S_{2}
be a formula substitution that substitutes
\Box(Q(x_{1})\vee Q(x_{2}))
for
P(x_{1},x_{2})
in
\mathcal{L}
-formulas.

Lemma 4.10

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic such that
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq L
. Then, for every
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi\in\mathbf{QCl}&\mathrel{\Longrightarrow}&S_{2}\varphi\in L.\end{array}

If
\varphi\in\mathbf{QCl}
, then
\varphi\in L
(since
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq L
), and
S_{2}\varphi\in L
by Substitution.

Lemma 4.11

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic such that
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is a wKHC class. Then, for every
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi
, containing no predicate letters except the binary letter
P
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}&\mathrel{\Longrightarrow}&S_{2}\varphi\not\in L_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}.\end{array}

Assume that
\varphi\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
.
Then, there exists a finite classical model
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
such that
\bm{M}\models\bm{sib}
and
\bm{M}\not\models\varphi
.
Let
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
be a Kripke frame and
w_{0}
a world such that
|R(w_{0})\setminus\{w_{0}\}|\geqslant|\mathcal{D}|^{2}
; such a frame exists, since
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is a wKHC class. Then
R(w_{0})\setminus\{w_{0}\}
contains a subset
W^{\prime}=\{w_{ij}:i,j\in\mathcal{D}\}
, where
w_{ij}=w_{kl}
if, and only if,
i=k
and
j=l
.
Let
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{F}\odot\mathcal{D},I\rangle
be a Kripke model such that, for every
w\in W
and every
a\in\mathcal{D}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\mathfrak{M},w\models Q(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{there is no $i,j\in\mathcal{D}$ such that $w=w_{ij}$,
$a\in\{i,j\}$, and $\bm{M}\not\models P(i,j)$.}\end{array}

Notice that such a model exists, since
\bm{M}\models\bm{sib}
.
Then, for all
a,b\in\mathcal{D}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\bm{M}\models P(a,b)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models\Box(Q(a)\vee Q(b)).\end{array}

As a result,
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\models S_{2}\varphi
, hence
S_{2}\varphi\not\in L_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
.

Theorem 4.12

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal predicate logics such that
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L^{\prime}
is a wKHC class. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Follows from Theorem
3.18
, Lemma
4.10
, and Lemma
4.11
.

Indeed, if
\varphi\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
, then, by Lemma
4.10
,
S_{2}\varphi\in L
; if
\varphi\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
, then, by Lemma
4.10
,
S_{2}\varphi\in L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
. Therefore, assuming that
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively separable, we obtain that
\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
should be recursively separable, which contradicts Theorem
3.18
.

Corollary 4.13

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal predicate logics such that
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L^{\prime}
is a wKHC class. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables.

If
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}
are recursively separable, then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
should be recursively separable, since
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}\subseteq L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
, which contradicts Theorem
4.12
.

Lemma 4.14

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic such that
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is a Skvortsov class. Then, for every
\mathcal{L}
-formula
\varphi
, containing no predicate letters except the binary letter
P
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}&\mathrel{\Longrightarrow}&S_{2}\varphi\not\in L_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}.\end{array}

Similar to the proof of Lemma
4.11
with the difference that the corresponding Kripke frame should be finite; such a frame exists since
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is a Skvortsov class.

Theorem 4.15

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal predicate logics such that
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L^{\prime}
is a Skvortsov class. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Follows from Theorem
3.18
, Lemma
4.10
, and Lemma
4.14
.

Corollary 4.16

Let
L
and
L^{\prime}
be modal predicate logics such that
\mathbf{QCl}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L^{\prime}
is a Skvortsov class. Then
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Similar to the proof of Corollary
4.13
.

Theorem
4.15
encompasses all the results outlined in
[
44
]
. Additionally, it extends its scope to include logics such as
\mathbf{QK5}
,
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
,
\mathbf{QK4B}
, as well as logics containing formulas that limit the depth or width of Kripke frames. It is worth noting that the methodology employed in Theorem
4.15
is not universally applicable to all the mentioned logics.

Let
\bm{bd}_{n}
and
\bm{bw}_{n}
be the formulas bounding, respectively, the depth and the width of Kripke frames by
n
, see
[
7
, Propositions 3.42–3.44]
.

Corollary 4.17

Let
L
be one of

\mathbf{QK}
,
\mathbf{QT}
,
\mathbf{QD}
,
\mathbf{QKB}
,
\mathbf{QKTB}
,
\mathbf{QK4}
,
\mathbf{QS4}
,
\mathbf{QK5}
,
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
,
\mathbf{QK4B}
,
\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}
,
\mathbf{QK4}\oplus\bm{bd}_{n}
,
\mathbf{QS4}\oplus\bm{bd}_{n}
,
\mathbf{QK4}\oplus\bm{bw}_{m}
,
\mathbf{QS4}\oplus\bm{bw}_{m}
,

\mathbf{QGL}\oplus\bm{bd}_{n}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}\oplus\bm{bd}_{n}
,
\mathbf{QGL}\oplus\bm{bw}_{m}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}\oplus\bm{bw}_{m}
,
where
n\in\mathds{N}^{++}
and
m\in\mathds{N}^{+}
.
Then
L
and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables, and the same for
L
and
L_{\mathit{dfin}}
.

Remark 4.18

Notice that Theorems
4.12
and
4.15
, as well as Corollaries
4.13
,
4.16
, and
4.17
, remain true if we additionally require that the language does not contain
\bot
(and hence,
\neg
and
\Diamond
as well).

Use Theorem
3.33
and the definition of
S_{2}
.

4.4
Examples

We give some examples of logics with certain algorithmic properties to draw attention to a number of points that may not be obvious or may not directly follow from the stated results.

Let us start with
a separating example for logics
L_{\mathit{dfin}}
and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
, for some modal predicate logic
L
; moreover, we shall show that even the monadic fragments with a single unary predicate letter and a single individual variable can be different. To this end, let us take
L=\mathbf{QS4}
and use a formula from
[
67
]
. Define

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{InfW}&=&\Box\exists x\,\neg Q(x)\wedge\Box\forall x\,(\neg Q(x)\to\Diamond Q(x))\wedge\Box\forall x\,(Q(x)\to\Box Q(x)).\end{array}

We can not refute
\neg\mathit{InfW}
on a finite Kripke frame validating
\mathbf{QS4}
.
Indeed, let
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
be a Kripke model with a reflexive transitive accessibility relation
R
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models\mathit{InfW}
, for some
w_{0}\in W
. Then there exist
w_{1}\in R(w_{0})
and
a_{1}\in D_{w_{1}}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{1}\models\neg Q(a_{1})
. Then
\mathfrak{M},w_{1}\models\neg Q(a_{1})\to\Diamond Q(a_{1})
, and hence,
\mathfrak{M},w_{1}\models\Diamond Q(a_{1})
. Then there exist
w_{2}\in R(w_{1})
and
a_{2}\in D_{w_{2}}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{2}\models\Box Q(a_{1})\wedge\neg Q(a_{2})
. Since
\mathfrak{M},w_{2}\models\neg Q(a_{2})
, repeating the same steps for
a_{2}
, we conclude that there exist
w_{3}\in R(w_{2})
and
a_{3}\in D_{w_{3}}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{3}\models\Box Q(a_{2})\wedge\neg Q(a_{3})
. Reasoning in the same way, we come to the conclusion that there are worlds
w_{1},w_{2},w_{3},\ldots{}
and individuals
a_{1},a_{2},a_{3},\ldots{}
such that, for every
k\in\mathds{N}^{+}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}w_{k}Rw_{k+1}&\mbox{and}&\mathfrak{M},w_{k+1}\models\Box Q(a_{k})\wedge\neg Q(a_{k+1}).\end{array}

It should be clear that
w_{i}\neq w_{j}
if
i\neq j
, and hence,
W
is infinite. Thus,
\neg\mathit{InfW}\in\mathbf{QS4}_{\mathit{wfin}}
. To show that
\neg\mathit{InfW}\not\in\mathbf{QS4}_{\mathit{dfin}}
, consider a Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathds{N},\leqslant,D,I\rangle
such that
D_{k}=\{0,\ldots,k\}
, for every
k\in\mathds{N}
, and

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},k\models Q(m)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&m<k,\end{array}

for every
k\in\mathds{N}
and every
m\in D_{k}
.
Then,
\mathfrak{M},0\models\mathit{InfW}
. Since
\mathfrak{M}
is based on a reflexive transitive Kripke frame and all its local domains are finite,
12
12

12

Observe that the global domain of
\mathfrak{M}
is infinite.

\mathfrak{M}\models\mathbf{QS4}_{\mathit{dfin}}
. Thus,
\neg\mathit{InfW}\not\in\mathbf{QS4}_{\mathit{dfin}}
.

Observe that it is not hard to expand this example to other modal predicate logics. For example, to expand it on
\mathbf{QK4}
, replace
\Box
with
\Box^{+}
, where
\Box^{+}\varphi=\varphi\wedge\Box\varphi
;
we leave the details to the reader.

Let us give separating examples for Proposition
4.3
and Theorem
4.12
as well as Proposition
4.6
and Theorem
4.15
. Dummy examples are logics
\mathbf{QTriv}
and
\mathbf{QVer}
. Since both logics satisfy the conditions of Propositions
4.3
and
4.6
, the fragments with a single binary predicate letter and three variables of

\mathbf{QTriv}
and
\mathbf{QTriv}_{\mathit{dfin}}
,

\mathbf{QTriv}
and
\mathbf{QTriv}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,

\mathbf{QVer}
and
\mathbf{QVer}_{\mathit{dfin}}
,

\mathbf{QVer}
and
\mathbf{QVer}_{\mathit{wfin}}
are recursively inseparable. Theorems
4.12
and
4.15
say nothing about the recursive separability of the monadic fragments of the same pairs of logics.
Note that the monadic fragments of all these logics are decidable (they are equivalent, for each of the logics, to the monadic fragment of
\mathbf{QCl}
). Therefore, it is clear that such fragments of logics from the same pairs are recursively separable.
So, let us give less trivial examples.

To this end, let us make an obvious observation, cf.
[
37
,
49
,
50
]
.

Proposition 4.19

Let
\mathfrak{F}
be a finite Kripke frame. Then the monadic fragments of the logics
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathfrak{F}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathfrak{F}
are decidable.

We give a sketch of a proof; for more details, see
[
37
,
49
,
50
]
.

Let
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
. Let
\varphi
be an
\mathcal{ML}
-formula containing no predicate letters except unary letters
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\models\varphi
, for some Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
and world
w_{0}\in W
. Define the equivalence relation
\sim
on
D^{+}
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}a\sim b&\mathrel{\leftrightharpoons}&\left\{\begin{array}[]{rcl}\{w\in W:a\in D_{w}\}&\!\!=&\{w\in W:b\in D_{w}\};\\
\{w\in W:\mathfrak{M},w\models P(a)\}&\!\!=&\{w\in W:\mathfrak{M},w\models P(b)\},\end{array}\right.\end{array}

and then define

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\bar{a}&=&\{b\in D^{+}:b\sim a\};\\
D^{\prime}_{w}&=&\{\bar{b}:b\in D_{w}\};\\
I^{\prime}(w,P_{k})&=&\{\langle\bar{b}\rangle:\langle b\rangle\in I(w,P_{k})\};\\
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}&=&\langle W,R,D^{\prime},I^{\prime}\rangle,\end{array}

where
a\in D^{+}
,
w\in W
, and
k\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
Then it is not hard to prove that
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w_{0}\not\models\varphi
.

Observe that the domain of
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
contains at most
2^{|W|\cdot(n+1)}
elements. Thus, to check whether
\varphi\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathfrak{F}
, it is sufficient to check whether
\varphi
is true in all such models; it is similar for
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathfrak{F}
.

So, due to Proposition
4.19
, we may replace the logics
\mathbf{QTriv}
and
\mathbf{QTriv}
above with
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathfrak{F}
or
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathfrak{F}
, where
\mathfrak{F}
is a finite Kripke frame.

Let us turn to logics not definable by a finite Kripke frame.

Consider, for each
n\in\mathds{N}
, the logics
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}
and
\mathbf{QTAlt}_{n}
defined by

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}&=&\mathbf{QK}\oplus\bm{alt}_{n};\\
\mathbf{QTAlt}_{n}&=&\mathbf{QT}\hfill\oplus\bm{alt}_{n},\end{array}

where

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bm{alt}_{n}&=&\displaystyle\neg\bigwedge\limits_{\mathclap{i=0}}^{n}\Diamond\Big(p_{i}\wedge\bigwedge\limits_{\mathclap{j\neq i}}\neg p_{j}\Big).\end{array}

The class
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}}
consists of
n
-alternative
Kripke frames, i.e., where each world in a frame sees at most
n
worlds; the same for
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QTAlt}_{n}}
, where the frames are both
n
-alternative and reflexive.
It is known that
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}
and
\mathbf{QTAlt}_{n}
are Kripke complete
[
52
,
53
]
, therefore, we obtain the following statement from Proposition
4.19
, cf.
[
37
,
49
,
50
]
.

Proposition 4.20

The monadic fragments of
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}
,
\mathbf{QTAlt}_{n}
,
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}\mathbf{.bf}
, and
\mathbf{QTAlt}_{n}\mathbf{.bf}
are decidable.

We give just a sketch of the proof for
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}
; for more details, see
[
37
,
49
,
50
]
.

Indeed, let
\varphi
be an
\mathcal{ML}
-formula containing no predicate letters except unary letters
P_{1},\ldots,P_{n}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\models\varphi
, for some Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
based on an
n
-alternative Kripke frame
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
and some world
w_{0}\in W
.
Let
m
be the modal depth
13
13

13

Largest number of nested modalities in the formula.
of
\varphi
.
Delete all worlds from
\mathfrak{M}
except

\{w\in W:\mbox{$w_{0}R^{k}w$, for some $k\leqslant m$}\},

and denote the resulting Kripke model by
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
. It is not hard to see that
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
is based on an
n
-alternative Kripke frame of depth at most
m+1
, therefore, the number of worlds in
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
does not exceed the number

1+n+n^{2}+\ldots+n^{m+1},

and
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\models\varphi
. Thus, to check whether
\varphi\in\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}
, it is sufficient to check whether
\varphi
is valid on the
n
-alternative Kripke frame of depth
m+1
, which is possible by Proposition
4.19
.

Notice that, by the proof,

\begin{array}[]{lclcl}\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}&=&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}}&=&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}};\\
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}\mathbf{.bf}&=&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}}&=&\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}},\end{array}

and, in fact, we proved the dicidability of the monadic fragments of
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}}
; the decidability of the corresponding fragments of
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}
and
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}\mathbf{.bf}
then follows from the Kripke completeness of the logics
[
52
,
53
]
. Without Kripke completeness, we could talk about the recursive separability of
\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{\mathbf{QAlt}_{n}}
. Let us generalize this observation.

A modal predicate logic
L
is called
subframe
if the class
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is closed under taking subframes
14
14

14

A Kripke frame
\langle W^{\prime},R^{\prime}\rangle
is a
subframe
of a Kripke frame
\langle W,R\rangle
if
W^{\prime}\subseteq W
and
R^{\prime}=R\upharpoonright W^{\prime}
.
of its Kripke frames. For example,
\mathbf{QK}
,
\mathbf{QT}
,
\mathbf{QK4}
,
\mathbf{QS4}
,
\mathbf{QS5}
are subframe logics and
\mathbf{QD}
,
\mathbf{QKD4}
are not.

Proposition 4.21

Let
L
be a subframe modal predicate logic such that

\mathbf{QK}\subseteq L
,

L\subseteq\mathbf{QTriv}
or
L\subseteq\mathbf{QVer}
,
and the class
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{L}
is recursive.
Then the monadic fragments of
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}(L\oplus\bm{alt}_{n})
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}(L\oplus\bm{alt}_{n})
are decidable.

Similar to Proposition
4.20
.
The only difference is that we also have to check that the submodel corresponding to
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}
in the proof of Proposition
4.20
also validates
L
. This is possible since
L
is a subframe logic and
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{L}
is a recursive class.

Corollary 4.22

Let
L
be a subframe modal predicate logic such that

\mathbf{QK}\subseteq L
,

L\subseteq\mathbf{QTriv}
or
L\subseteq\mathbf{QVer}
,
and the class
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}{L}
is recursive.
Then the monadic fragments of
L\oplus\bm{alt}_{n}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}(L\oplus\bm{alt}_{n})
are recursively separable.

Corollary 4.23

Let
L
be a one of
\mathbf{QK}
,
\mathbf{QT}
,
\mathbf{QKB}
,
\mathbf{QKTB}
,
\mathbf{QK4}
,
\mathbf{QS4}
,
\mathbf{QK5}
,
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QK4B}
,
\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}
,
\mathbf{QwGrz}
,
\mathbf{QK4}\oplus\bm{bd}_{m}
,
\mathbf{QS4}\oplus\bm{bd}_{m}
,
\mathbf{QGL}\oplus\bm{bd}_{m}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}\oplus\bm{bd}_{m}
,
\mathbf{QwGrz}\oplus\bm{bd}_{m}
, where
m\in\mathds{N}^{+}
.
Then the monadic fragments of
L\oplus\bm{alt}_{n}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}(L\oplus\bm{alt}_{n})
are recursively separable.

Now, let us pay attention to the fact that the logics under consideration are not necessarily in
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
or
\Pi^{0}_{1}
.
Of course, by Propositions
3.23
and
3.26
, each of two recursively inseparable logics (or its fragment) with which we deal is hard in some of the classes; however, this does not necessarily imply membership within the class. We give some examples.
By Theorem
4.12
, the monadic fragments of
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathbf{QGL}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathbf{QGL}
are recursively inseparable; and, by Theorem
4.15
, the same for the monadic fragments of
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathbf{QGL}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathbf{QGL}
. The monadic fragments of
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathbf{QGL}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathbf{QGL}
are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-complete by
[
49
, Corollary 4.3]
. But the monadic fragments of
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathbf{QGL}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathbf{QGL}
are
\Pi^{1}_{1}
-hard
[
31
]
(in particular,
\mathbf{QGL}\neq\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\mathbf{QGL}
).
The same applies to
\mathbf{QGrz}
,
\mathbf{QwGrz}
, and some closely related logics considered in
[
31
]
.

Let us give examples where the conditions of Theorems
4.12
and
4.15
are not satisfied for logics
L
and
L^{\prime}
but the monadic fragments of
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}
as well as the monadic fragments of
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}
are recursively inseparable. In
[
42
]
, for any degree of unsolvability
A
, a linearly approximable propositional logic
\mathbf{L}_{A}
with the variable-free fragment belonging to the degree is constructed. By construction, the logic contains
\bm{alt}_{2}
(and
\Box\bm{alt}_{1}
); therefore,
\mathbf{QL}_{A}
does not satisfy the conditions of Theorems
4.12
and
4.15
(if we take
L=L^{\prime}=\mathbf{L}_{A}
). However, the monadic fragments of
\mathbf{QL}_{A}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathbf{QL}_{A}
as well as the monadic fragments of
\mathbf{QL}_{A}
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathbf{QL}_{A}
are not recursively separable, since even the propositional variable-free fragments of the logics in the pairs are the same and undecidable.

Finally, we pay attention to very poor logical systems. As an example let us consider the modal predicate logic
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}
defined by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}&=&\mathbf{QCl}+\Box\top\to\Box\top,\end{array}

i.e.,
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}
is, in fact, the classical logic whose language is enriched with
\Box
without any condition on
\Box
. Clearly,
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}
is contained in every modal predicate logic containing
\mathbf{QCl}
. At first glance, it seems inappropriate to talk about the logics
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{dfin}}
and
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{wfin}}
, since, by the definitions, they coincide with
\mathbf{QK}_{\mathit{dfin}}
and
\mathbf{QK}_{\mathit{wfin}}
, respectively; but it seems appropriate to consider
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}
defined by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}&=&\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}+\Box\top\to\Box\top.\end{array}

Note that, at least,
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}\subseteq\mathbf{QK}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
, since
\mathbf{QK}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}\cap\mathcal{L}=\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
.
15
15

15

The logics are different. For example,
\Box\top\in\mathbf{QK}_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
but
\Box\top\not\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}
. We leave the details to the reader.
Then as a corollary we obtain the following proposition.

Proposition 4.24

Logics
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}
and
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in a language containing a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables.

Let
\varphi
be an
\mathcal{L}
-formula containing no predicate letters except a binary letter
P
and containing just three individual variables.

If
\varphi\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\mathit{bin}}
, then
S_{2}\varphi\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}
by Substitution.

Suppose
\varphi\not\in\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
. We are to prove that
S_{2}\varphi\not\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}
. Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that
S_{2}\varphi\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}
. Since
\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}\subseteq\mathbf{QK}_{\mathit{dfin}}
, we conclude that
S_{2}\varphi\in\mathbf{QK}_{\mathit{dfin}}
, which contradicts Lemma
4.11
. Thus,
S_{2}\varphi\not\in\mathbf{QCl}^{\Box}_{\mathit{fin}}
.

Then, the statement follows by Theorem
3.18
.

Notice that a similar statement can be proved for the logics
\mathbf{QCl}+\Box\top
and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}+\Box\top
as well as
\mathbf{QCl}\oplus\top
and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}\oplus\top
, and many others. We leave the details to the reader.

4.5
Monadic fragments and two variables

It is known that the monadic fragments of modal logics can be undecidable even if the language contains only two individual variables
[
22
]
and a single unary predicate letter
[
43
]
. For the logics of classes of finite frames, a similar result is proved with the use of three individual variables
[
44
]
. Using the ideas presented in
[
22
]
we shall show that, for many modal predicate logics, the fragment of a logic with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables and the one of the logic of its finite Kripke frames are recursively inseparable. To this end, we can not use the results on theories like
\mathbf{SIB}
and
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
directly, since they are decidable in languages with fewer than three individual variables; therefore, we are going to describe the special
T_{n}
-tiling, for each
n\in\mathds{N}
, by
\mathcal{ML}
-formulas containing a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables. To simulate the special
T_{n}
-tiling in an appropriate way, let us slightly modify the constructions proposed in
[
22
]
.

Below, we shall use the formulas and denotations introduced in Section
3.4
. We define a formula similar to
\mathit{Tiling}^{\prime}_{n}
using
\Box
and just two individual variables. Let
C
be a new unary predicate letter. Define (cf.
[
22
]
) the following
\mathcal{ML}
-formulas:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{3}&=&\Box\forall x\forall y\,(V^{\prime}_{n}(x,y)\wedge\exists x\,(C(x)\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(y,x))\to\forall y\,(H^{\prime}_{n}(x,y)\to\forall x\,(C(x)\to V^{\prime}_{n}(y,x))));\\
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{5}&=&\forall x\,\Diamond C(x);\\
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{6}&=&\forall x\forall y\,(V^{\prime}_{n}(x,y)\to\Box V^{\prime}_{n}(x,y));\\
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{7}&=&\forall x\forall y\,(H^{\prime}_{n}(x,y)\to\Box H^{\prime}_{n}(x,y));\\
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{8}&=&\forall x\forall y\,(\Diamond V^{\prime}_{n}(x,y)\to V^{\prime}_{n}(x,y)).\end{array}

Letter
C
allows us to replace
\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{3}
, which contains three individual variables, with
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{3}
that contains two individual variables; we need
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{5}
–
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{8}
to ensure that
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{3}
works properly. Let, for convenience,

\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{0}=\mathit{TC}_{0},&\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{1}=\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{1},&\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{2}=\mathit{TC}^{\prime}_{2},&\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{4}=\mathit{TC}_{4}.\end{array}

Then, define

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathit{Tiling}^{\Box}_{n}&=&\displaystyle\bigwedge\limits_{\mathclap{i=0}}^{8}\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{i}\wedge\Box^{+}\mathit{DSR}\wedge\Box^{+}\mathit{DSU}.\end{array}

Finally, we define a new, modal, modification of
\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathit{M}^{\Box}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}&=&\mathit{Tiling}^{\Box}_{n}\to\exists x\,P_{1}(x).\\
\end{array}

Lemma 4.25

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathit{M}^{\Box}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QK}
.

Let
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models\mathit{Tiling}_{n}
for a Kripke model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
and a world
w_{0}\in W
. We show that then
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models\exists x\,P_{1}(x)
.
To this end, for all
i,j\in\mathds{N}
we pick out an element
a_{i}^{j}\in D_{w_{0}}
so that, for all
i,j\in\mathds{N}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a_{i}^{j},a_{i+1}^{j})&\mbox{and}&\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models V^{\prime}_{n}(a_{i}^{j},a_{i}^{j+1}).\end{array}

Since
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{4}
, there exists
a_{0}^{0}\in D_{w_{0}}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models P_{0}(a_{0}^{0})
.

Let
k
be a number from
\mathds{N}
. Suppose that for all
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, the element
a_{i}^{j}
is defined; we have to define, for all
i,j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
, the elements
a_{k+1}^{j}
,
a_{i}^{k+1}
, and
a_{k+1}^{k+1}
.

We start with
a_{0}^{k+1}
. Due to
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{2}
, there exists
b\in D_{w_{0}}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models V^{\prime}_{n}(a_{0}^{k},b)
. Then take
a_{0}^{k+1}=b
. To define other elements, we first prove an auxiliary statement, cf.
[
22
]
.

Sublemma 4.26

Let
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a,c)\wedge V^{\prime}_{n}(a,e)\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(e,b)
, for some
a,c,e,b\in D_{w_{0}}
. Then
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models V^{\prime}_{n}(c,b)
.

Using
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{5}
, we obtain that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models\Diamond C(b)
, and hence, there exists a world
w\in R(w_{0})
such that,

\mathfrak{M},w\models C(b).

(4.1)

Since
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a,c)\wedge V^{\prime}_{n}(a,e)\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(e,b)
and
w_{0}Rw
, we obtain, by
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{6}
and
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{7}
, that

\mathfrak{M},w\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a,c)\wedge V^{\prime}_{n}(a,e)\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(e,b).

(4.2)

Now, let us use the formula
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{3}
. It follows from
(\ref{eq:star:1})
and
(\ref{eq:star:2})
that
\mathfrak{M},w\models C(b)\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(e,b)
, and thus,
\mathfrak{M},w\models\exists x\,(C(x)\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(e,x))
; then, applying
(\ref{eq:star:2})
again, we conclude that

\mathfrak{M},w\models V^{\prime}_{n}(a,e)\wedge\exists x\,(C(x)\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(e,x)).

(4.3)

By
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{3}
, from
(\ref{eq:star:3})
we obtain that

\mathfrak{M},w\models\forall y\,(H^{\prime}_{n}(a,y)\to\forall x\,(C(x)\wedge V^{\prime}_{n}(y,x)),

and hence,

\mathfrak{M},w\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a,c)\to\forall x\,(C(x)\wedge V^{\prime}_{n}(c,x)),

that, by
(\ref{eq:star:2})
, gives us

\mathfrak{M},w\models\forall x\,(C(x)\wedge V^{\prime}_{n}(c,x)),

and, by
(\ref{eq:star:1})
,

\mathfrak{M},w\models V^{\prime}_{n}(c,b).

(4.4)

Since
w_{0}Rw
, we obtain
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models\Diamond V^{\prime}_{n}(c,b)
from
(\ref{eq:star:4})
. Then, by
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{8}
, we can conclude that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models V^{\prime}_{n}(c,b)
.

Suppose that
a_{0}^{k+1},\ldots,a_{i}^{k+1}
are already defined for some
i\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
; we have to define
a_{i+1}^{k+1}
. Due to
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{1}
, there exists
b\in D_{w_{0}}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a_{i}^{k+1},b)
. Since also
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a_{i}^{k},a_{i+1}^{k})
and
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models V^{\prime}_{n}(a_{i}^{k},a_{i}^{k+1})
, we obtain, by Sublemma
4.26
, that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a_{i+1}^{k+1},b)
, and we may take
a_{i+1}^{k+1}=b
.

Next step, let us define
a_{k+1}^{k+1}
. By
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{1}
, there exists
b\in D_{w_{0}}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a_{k}^{k+1},b)
; take
a_{k+1}^{k+1}=b
.

Suppose that
a_{k+1}^{k+1},\ldots,a_{k+1}^{j+1}
are already defined for some
j\in\{0,\ldots,k\}
; we have to define
a_{k+1}^{j}
. By
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{1}
, there exists
c\in D_{w_{0}}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a_{k}^{j},c)
. Then,

\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H^{\prime}_{n}(a_{k}^{j},c)\wedge V^{\prime}_{n}(a_{k}^{j},a_{k}^{j+1})\wedge H^{\prime}_{n}(a_{k}^{j+1},a_{k+1}^{j+1}),

and by Sublemma
4.26
,
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models V^{\prime}_{n}(c,a_{k+1}^{j+1})
. Hence, we may take
a_{k+1}^{k+1}=c
.

For all
i,j\in\mathds{N}
, due to
\mathit{TC}^{\Box}_{0}
, there exists a unique
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models P_{m}(a_{i}^{j})
. Then, put
f(i,j)=t^{n}_{m}
.
Clearly,
f\colon\mathds{N}\times\mathds{N}\to T_{n}
is a
T_{n}
-tiling, since

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models H_{n}(a_{i}^{j},a_{i+1}^{j})&\Longrightarrow&\mathop{\hbox to5.52pt{\vbox to5.52pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 0.2pt\lower-0.2pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{}{{}}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{}{}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 0 L 0 7.09 L 7.09 7.09 L 7.09 0 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

Notice that
f(0,0)=t^{n}_{0}=t_{0}
, since
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models P_{0}(a_{0}^{0})
. Then, by Proposition
2.1
,
f
is the special
T_{n}
-tiling
f_{n}
. Since
n\in\mathbb{X}
, by
(\ref{eq:fn})
we obtain that there exists
m\in\mathds{N}
such that
f_{n}(0,m)=t_{1}=t^{n}_{1}
. This means that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models P_{1}(a_{0}^{m})
, and hence,
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models\exists x\,P_{1}(x)
.

Thus,
\mathit{M}^{\Box}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QK}
.

The next aim is to eliminate almost all unary predicate letters by simulating them with formulas containing the binary letter
P
and two individual variables. Let us make a relativization. Let
G
be a new unary predicate letter and, as before,

\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\forall_{G}x\,\varphi&=&\forall x\,(G(x)\to\varphi);\\
\exists_{G}x\,\varphi&=&\exists x\,(G(x)\hfill\wedge\hfill\varphi).\end{array}

Also, for an
\mathcal{ML}
-formula
\varphi
, denote by
\varphi_{G}
the formula obtained from
\varphi
by replacing each quantifier
\forall x
or
\exists x
with
\forall_{G}x
or
\exists_{G}x
, respectively.

Define

\begin{array}[]{lclclcl}\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}&=&\exists x\,G(x)\wedge\forall x\,(G(x)\to\Box G(x))\to(\mathit{M}^{\Box}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}.\end{array}

Lemma 4.27

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QK}
.

The statement should be clear; we just give a sketch of the proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{X}
. Suppose that
\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QK}
. Consequently, there exist a model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
and a world
w\in W
such that
\mathfrak{M},w\not\models\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
. Consider the model
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=\langle W^{\prime},R^{\prime},D^{\prime},I^{\prime}\rangle
defined by

\begin{array}[]{rcll}W^{\prime}&=&\{w\}\cup R(w);\\
R^{\prime}&=&R\upharpoonright W^{\prime};\\
D^{\prime}_{v}&=&\{a\in D_{v}:\mathfrak{M},v\models G(a)\},&\mbox{where $v\in W^{\prime}$;}\\
I^{\prime}(v,E)&=&I(v,E)\upharpoonright D^{\prime}_{v},&\mbox{where $v\in W^{\prime}$ and $E$ is a predicate letter.}\end{array}

Then it is not hard to check that
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime},w\not\models\mathit{M}^{\Box}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
; we leave the details to the reader. This contradicts Lemma
4.25
, and therefore,
\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QK}
.

Let us define a function
S_{3}
, which is similar to
S_{1}
. Define
S_{3}\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
as the formula obtained from
\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
by replacing

•

each occurrence of
P_{m}(x)
and
P_{m}(y)
with
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{m}(x)
and
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{m}(y)
, respectively, where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
;

•

then each occurrence of
R_{i}(x)
and
R_{i}(y)
with
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+i}(x)
and
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+i}(y)
, respectively, where
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
;

•

and then each occurrence of
U_{j}(x)
and
U_{j}(y)
with
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(x)
and
\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(y)
, respectively, where
1\leqslant j\leqslant 4
.

Notice that these replacements are formula substitutions.
16
16

16

See footnote
3
.

Observe that, for every
n\in\mathds{N}
, formula
S_{3}\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
contains two individual variables and does not contain predicate letters except
P
,
C
, and
G
.

Lemma 4.28

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
S_{3}\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QK}
.

Immediately follows from Lemma
4.27
, since
\mathbf{QK}
is closed under Substitution.

Lemma 4.29

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic such that
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is a wKHC class. Then,
n\in\mathbb{Y}
implies

S_{3}\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.

We are going to apply an argumentation similar to that used in the proof of Lemma
3.17
. In fact, the difference is that we do not need to simulate
G
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Y}
.
Then, by Lemma
3.10
,
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}\uplus\bm{sib}
. Hence,
there exists a finite classical model
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
such that
\bm{M}\models\bm{sib}
and
\bm{M}\not\models\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
; we may assume that
\bm{M}
is the model defined in the proof of Lemma
3.10
, in particular,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{D}&=&\{0,\ldots,r+4\}\times\{0,\ldots,r+4\},\\
\end{array}

for a suitable
r\in\mathds{N}
.

For every
a\in\mathcal{D}
, take the elements

e_{0}^{a},\ldots,e_{k_{n}+10}^{a}
and
e_{P}^{a},e_{R}^{a},e_{U}^{a}
; let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}&=&\mathcal{D}\cup\{e_{0}^{a},\ldots,e_{k_{n}+10}^{a},e_{P}^{a},e_{R}^{a},e_{U}^{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}.\end{array}

Let
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
be a Kripke frame from
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
with a world
w_{0}
such that
|R(w_{0})\setminus\{w_{0}\}|\geqslant|\mathcal{D}|
; such a frame exists, since
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is a wKHC class. Then
R(w_{0})\setminus\{w_{0}\}
contains a subset
W^{\prime}=\{w_{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}
, where
w_{a}=w_{b}
if, and only if,
a=b
.
Let
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{F}\odot\mathcal{D}^{\prime},I\rangle
be a Kripke model such that, for every
w\in W
and every
a\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w\models G(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&a\in\mathcal{D};\\
\mathfrak{M},w\models C(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&w=w_{a},\end{array}

and, for every
w\in W
,
I(w,P)
is the symmetric closure of the relation

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{I}(P)\cup\{\langle a,e_{0}^{a}\rangle,\langle e_{0}^{a},e_{1}^{a}\rangle,\ldots,\langle e_{k_{n}+9}^{a},e_{k_{n}+10}^{a}\rangle:a\in\mathcal{D}\}\\
\phantom{\mathcal{I}(P)}\cup\{\langle e_{m}^{a},e_{P}^{a}\rangle:\mbox{$m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}$ and $\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a)$}\}\\
\phantom{\mathcal{I}(P)}\cup\{\langle e_{k_{n}+i}^{a},e_{R}^{a}\rangle:\mbox{$i\in\{1,2,3,4\}$ and $\bm{M}\models R_{i}(a)$}\}\\
\phantom{\mathcal{I}(P)}\cup\{\langle e_{k_{n}+j+4}^{a},e_{U}^{a}\rangle:\mbox{$j\in\{1,2,3,4\}$ and $\bm{M}\models U_{j}(a)$}\}.\end{array}

see Figure
16
.
Then it should be clear that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\models S_{3}\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
; we leave the details to the reader.

Thus,
S_{3}\mathit{M}^{\Box}_{G}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.
