\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}f(i,j+1).\end{array}

Notice that
f(0,0)=t^{n}_{0}=t_{0}
, since
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\models P_{0}(a_{0}^{0})
. Then, by Proposition
2.1
,
f
is the special
T_{n}
-tiling
f_{n}
. Since
n\in\mathbb{X}
, by
(\ref{eq:fn})
we obtain that there exists
m\in\mathds{N}
such that
f_{n}(0,m)=t_{1}=t^{n}_{1}
. This means that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}P_{1}(a_{0}^{m})
, and hence,
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}\exists x\,P_{1}(x)
. But
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}\exists x\,P_{1}(x)
by (
5.3
), hence, we obtain a contradiction.

Thus,
\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QInt}
.

Lemma 5.15

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic such that
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is an swKHC class. If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QSIL}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Y}
. By Lemma
3.10
,
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}\uplus\bm{sib}
. Let
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
be a finite model such that
\bm{M}\not\models\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
and
\bm{M}\models\bm{sib}
; we may assume that
\bm{M}
is the model constructed for
n
in the proof of Lemma
3.10
.

Since
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is an swKHC class, there exists an intuitionistic Kripke frame
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
in it such that, for some
w_{0}\in W
, the set
R(w_{0})
contains an antichain with at least
|\mathcal{D}|
worlds. Let
W^{\prime}=\{w_{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}
be a subset of the antichain with
w_{a}\neq w_{b}
whenever
a\neq b
. Let us consider a model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{F}\odot\mathcal{D},I\rangle
such that, for every
w\in W
, every
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
, all
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, and all
a,b\in\mathcal{D}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}P(a,b)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$R(w)\cap W^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $\bm{M}\models P(a,b)$;}\\
\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}P_{m}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$R(w)\cap W^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a)$;}\\
\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}R_{i}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$R(w)\cap W^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $\bm{M}\models R_{i}(a)$;}\\
\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}U_{j}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$R(w)\cap W^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $\bm{M}\models U_{j}(a)$;}\\
\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}C\phantom{{}_{i}}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$R(w)\cap W^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $w=w_{a}$.}\end{array}

(5.4)

Then, it is not hard to show that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
; we leave the details to the reader.

Hence,
\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QSIL}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.

Lemma 5.16

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic such that
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is an swKHC class. If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QSIL}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
5.15
with the difference that the corresponding intuitionistic Kripke frame must be finite; such a frame exists in
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
, since it is an swKHC class.

Let us define a function
S_{8}
, which is similar to the composition of
S_{7}
,
S_{6}
, and
S^{\prime}_{3}
. Define
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
as the formula obtained from
\exists x\,G(x)\to(\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}
by replacing

•

each occurrence of
P_{m}(x)
and
P_{m}(y)
with
(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{m}(x))^{+}
and
(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{m}(y))^{+}
, respectively, where
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
;

•

then each occurrence of
R_{i}(x)
and
R_{i}(y)
with
(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+i}(x))^{+}
and
(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+i}(y))^{+}
, respectively, where
1\leqslant i\leqslant 4
;

•

then each occurrence of
U_{j}(x)
and
U_{j}(y)
with
(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(x))^{+}
and
(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(y))^{+}
, respectively, where
1\leqslant j\leqslant 4
;

•

then each occurrence of
C(x)
and
C(y)
with
(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+9}(x))^{+}
and
(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+9}(y))^{+}
, respectively;

•

then each occurrence of
P(x_{1},x_{2})
with
(Q(x_{1})\wedge Q(x_{2})\to\forall x\,Q(x))\vee\forall x\,G(x)
, where
x_{1},x_{2}\in\{x,y\}
.

Notice that
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
is a positive formula that contains just two individual variables (
x
and
y
) and just two predicate letters (
Q
and
G
) which are unary.

Lemma 5.17

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QInt}
.

It should be clear; we give just a sketch of a proof.

Let
n\in\mathbb{X}
. Suppose that
\exists x\,G(x)\to(\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}\not\in\mathbf{QInt}
. Then there exist an intuitionistic model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle W,R,D,I\rangle
and a world
w\in W
such that
\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}\exists x\,G(x)
and
\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}(\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}})_{G}
. Let
\mathfrak{M}^{\prime}=\langle W^{\prime},R^{\prime},D^{\prime},I^{\prime}\rangle
be the model defined by

\begin{array}[]{rcll}W^{\prime}&=&R(w);\\
R^{\prime}&=&R\upharpoonright W^{\prime}\\
D^{\prime}_{v}&=&\{a\in D_{v}:\mathfrak{M},v\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}G(a)\},&\mbox{where $v\in W^{\prime}$};\\
I^{\prime}(v,E)&=&I(v,E)\upharpoonright D^{\prime}_{v},&\mbox{where $v\in W^{\prime}$ and $E$ is a predicate letter}.\\
\end{array}

Then it should be not hard to show that
\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
, that contradictis to Lemma
5.13
.

Finally, observe that all
clauses in the definition of
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
define, in fact, formula substitutions; therefore, the resulting formula is in
\mathbf{QInt}
.

We say that a class
\mathscr{C}
of intuitionistic Kripke frames satisfies the
special weak Kontchakov–Kurucz–Zakharyaschev condition
(for short,
swKKZ
) if, for every
n\in\mathds{N}
, there exist a Kripke frame
\langle W,R\rangle\in\mathscr{C}
, a world
w\in W
such that
R(w)
contains an
R
-antichain with
n
elements and, for every
v
from the antichain,
R(v)
contains an
R
-antichain with
n
elements and satisfies the following property: for all different
v^{\prime}
and
v^{\prime\prime}
from the antichain in
R(w)
, there is no elements
u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime\prime}
in the antichains contained in
R(v^{\prime})
and
R(v^{\prime\prime})
, respectively, such that
u^{\prime}Ru^{\prime\prime}
or
u^{\prime\prime}Ru^{\prime}
.
Clearly, if a class
\mathscr{C}
of intuitionistic Kripke frames satisfies swKKZ, then it also satisfies both swKHC and wKKZ. Nevertheless, let us consider the class of all intuitionistic Kripke frames of the following form: a root sees an antichain consisting of worlds seeing the same chain of worlds (variation: the same antichain of worlds); it satisfies both swKHC and wKKZ but does not satisfy swKKZ.

Lemma 5.18

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic such that
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is an swKKZ class. If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QSIL}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.

We are going to apply an argumentation similar to that used in the proofs of Lemmas
4.40
,
5.2
, and
5.8
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Y}
.
Then, by Lemma
3.10
,
\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}\uplus\bm{sib}
; let
\bm{M}=\langle\mathcal{D},\mathcal{I}\rangle
be a classical model such that
\bm{M}\models\bm{sib}
and
\bm{M}\not\models\mathit{MTiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
; we may assume that
\bm{M}
is the model defined in the proof of Lemma
3.10
, in particular,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{D}&=&\{0,\ldots,r+4\}\times\{0,\ldots,r+4\},\\
\end{array}

for a suitable
r\in\mathds{N}
. As in the proof of Lemma
4.40
, let

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathcal{D}^{\prime}&=&\mathcal{D}\cup\{e_{0}^{a},\ldots,e_{k_{n}+11}^{a},e_{P}^{a},e_{R}^{a},e_{U}^{a},e_{C}^{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}.\end{array}

Since
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is an swKKZ class, it contains a Kripke frame
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}=\langle\bar{W},\bar{R}\rangle
with a world
\bar{w}_{0}\in\bar{W}
such that
\bar{R}(\bar{w}_{0})
contains an
\bar{R}
-antichain with
|\mathcal{D}|^{2}
elements and, for every
v
from the antichain,
\bar{R}(v)
contains an
\bar{R}
-antichain with
|\mathcal{D}|^{2}
elements, withal there are no elements
u^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime\prime}
from different the antichains satisfying
u^{\prime}Ru^{\prime\prime}
or
u^{\prime\prime}Ru^{\prime}
. Let
\bar{W}^{\prime}=\{\bar{w}_{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}
be a subset of the antichain contained in
\bar{R}(\bar{w}_{0})
and, for every
a\in\mathcal{D}
, let
U_{a}=\{u^{a}_{bc}:b,c\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\}
be a subset of the antichain contained in
\bar{R}(\bar{w}_{a})
; we assume that
\bar{w}_{a}\neq\bar{w}_{b}
whenever
a\neq b
and also
u^{a}_{bc}\neq u^{a}_{de}
whenever
\{b,c\}\neq\{d,e\}
. Let also

\begin{array}[]{lcl}U&=&\displaystyle\bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{a\in\mathcal{D}}}U_{a}.\\
\end{array}

Let
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}=\langle\bar{\mathfrak{F}}\odot\mathcal{D}^{\prime},\bar{I}\rangle
be a model defined so that

•

for every
w\in\bar{W}
and every
a\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}G(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$\bar{R}(w)\cap\bar{W}^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $a\in\mathcal{D}$;}\\
\end{array}

(5.5)

•

for every
w\in\bar{W}\setminus\bar{W}^{\prime}
such that
\bar{R}(w)\cap\bar{W}^{\prime}\neq\varnothing
, the relation
\bar{I}(w,P)
is the symmetric closure of the relation

\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{I}(P)\cup\{\langle a,e_{0}^{a}\rangle,\langle e_{0}^{a},e_{1}^{a}\rangle,\ldots,\langle e_{k_{n}+10}^{a},e_{k_{n}+11}^{a}\rangle:a\in\mathcal{D}\}\\
\phantom{\mathcal{I}(P)}\cup\{\langle e_{m}^{a},e_{P}^{a}\rangle:\mbox{$m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}$ and $\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a)$}\}\\
\phantom{\mathcal{I}(P)}\cup\{\langle e_{k_{n}+i}^{a},e_{R}^{a}\rangle:\mbox{$i\in\{1,2,3,4\}$ and $\bm{M}\models R_{i}(a)$}\}\\
\phantom{\mathcal{I}(P)}\cup\{\langle e_{k_{n}+j+4}^{a},e_{U}^{a}\rangle:\mbox{$j\in\{1,2,3,4\}$ and $\bm{M}\models U_{j}(a)$}\};\end{array}

•

for every
a\in\mathcal{D}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{I}(\bar{w}_{a},P)&=&I(w_{0},P)\cup\{\langle e_{k_{n}+9}^{a},e_{C}^{a}\rangle,\langle e_{C}^{a},e_{k_{n}+9}^{a}\rangle\};\end{array}

•

for every
w\in\bar{W}(w)
such that
\bar{R}(w)\cap\bar{W}^{\prime}=\varnothing
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{I}(w,P)&=&\mathcal{D}^{\prime}\times\mathcal{D}^{\prime};\end{array}

•

for every
a\in\mathcal{D}
and all
b,c,e\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{\mathfrak{M}},u^{a}_{bc}\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}Q(e)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$e\in\{b,c\}$ and $\bar{\mathfrak{M}},\bar{w}_{a}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}P(b,c)$;}\\
\end{array}

•

for every
w\in\bar{W}\setminus U
and every
e\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}Q(e)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$\bar{R}(w)\cap U=\varnothing$;}\\
\end{array}

see Figure
17
for a part of
\bar{I}(\bar{w}_{a},P)
, where
\neg C(b)
and
\neg C(c)
now mean that
\mathfrak{M},w_{a}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}C(b)
and
\mathfrak{M},w_{a}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}C(c)
for
\mathfrak{M}
defined in the proof of Lemma
5.15
.
Notice that, in fact, we are interested in the definitions for
\bar{I}(w,Q)
and
\bar{I}(w,G)
only; the definitions for
\bar{I}(w,P)
, where
w\in\bar{W}
,
are auxiliary.
Nevertheless, let us observe that, by the definition of model
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
, for every
w\in\bar{W}
and all
c,e\in\mathcal{D}^{\prime}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}P(c,e)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}(Q(c)\wedge Q(e)\to\forall x\,Q(x))\vee\forall x\,G(x).\\
\end{array}

(5.6)

Let us compare
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
and
\mathfrak{M}
, where
\mathfrak{M}
is the model defined in the proof of Lemma
5.15
. Observe that, for every
w\in\bar{W}
, every
m\in\{0,\ldots,k_{n}\}
, all
i,j\in\{1,2,3,4\}
, and all
a,b\in\mathcal{D}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}P(a,b)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$\bar{R}(w)\cap\bar{W}^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $\bm{M}\models P(a,b)$;}\\
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{m}(a))^{+}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$\bar{R}(w)\cap\bar{W}^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $\bm{M}\models P_{m}(a)$;}\\
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+i}(a))^{+}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$\bar{R}(w)\cap\bar{W}^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $\bm{M}\models R_{i}(a)$;}\\
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+j+4}(a))^{+}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$\bar{R}(w)\cap\bar{W}^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $\bm{M}\models U_{j}(a)$;}\\
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}(\mathit{tile}^{\prime}_{k_{n}+9}(a))^{+}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{$\bar{R}(w)\cap\bar{W}^{\prime}=\varnothing$ or $w=\bar{w}_{a}$,}\end{array}

(5.7)

where
\bm{M}
is the model defined in the proof of Lemma
3.10
.

Then, by (
5.4
)–(
5.7
),
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
implies that

\bar{\mathfrak{M}},\bar{w}_{0}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
.

Thus,
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QSIL}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.

Lemma 5.19

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic such that
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
is an swKKZ class. If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QSIL}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.

Similar to the proof of Lemma
5.18
with the difference that the corresponding intuitionistic Kripke frame must be finite; such a frame exists in
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
, since it is an swKKZ class; also, use the model mentioned in the proof of Lemma
5.16
instead of the model from the proof of Lemma
5.15
.

Theorem 5.20

The positive fragments of logics

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{QInt}&\mbox{and\/}&\mathbf{QInt}_{\mathit{dfin}}\hfill+\bm{bd}_{3}+\bm{cd},\\
\mathbf{QInt}&\mbox{and\/}&\mathbf{QInt}_{\mathit{wfin}}\hfill+\bm{bd}_{3}+\bm{cd},\\
\mathbf{QInt}&\mbox{and\/}&\mathbf{QKC}_{\mathit{dfin}}\hfill+\bm{bd}_{4}+\bm{cd},\\
\mathbf{QInt}&\mbox{and\/}&\mathbf{QKC}_{\mathit{wfin}}\hfill+\bm{bd}_{4}+\bm{cd}\end{array}

are recursively inseparable in the language with two unary predicate letters and two individual variables.

Follows from Lemmas
5.17
,
5.18
, and
5.19
.

Corollary 5.21

Let
\mathbf{QInt}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and also either
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{3}+\bm{cd}
or
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{4}+\bm{cd}
. Then the positive fragments of
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}
as well as
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with two unary predicate letters and two individual variables.

Corollary 5.22

Let
L
be one of
\mathbf{QInt}
,
\mathbf{QKP}
,
\mathbf{QKC}
,
\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{n}
with
n\geqslant 3
,
\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{n}
with
n\geqslant 4
. Then the positive fragments of
L
and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
as well as
L
and
L_{\mathit{dfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with two unary predicate letters and two individual variables.

Finally, let us eliminate predicate letters
Q
and
G
simulating them by formulas with a single unary predicate letter
P^{\prime}
. To this end, let us consider the following formulas (cf.
[
34
,
36
,
47
,
48
]
):

\begin{array}[]{lcl}D_{1}&=&\exists x\,P^{\prime}(x);\\
D_{2}(x)&=&\exists x\,P^{\prime}(x)\to P^{\prime}(x);\\
D_{3}(x)&=&P^{\prime}(x)\to\forall x\,P^{\prime}(x);\\
A_{1}^{0}(x)&=&D_{2}(x)\to D_{1}\vee D_{3}(x);\\
A_{2}^{0}(x)&=&D_{3}(x)\to D_{1}\vee D_{2}(x);\\
B_{1}^{0}(x)&=&D_{1}\to D_{2}(x)\vee D_{3}(x);\\
B_{2}^{0}(x)&=&A_{1}^{0}(x)\wedge A_{2}^{0}(x)\wedge B_{1}^{0}(x)\to D_{1}\vee D_{2}(x)\vee D_{3}(x);\\
A_{1}^{1}(x)&=&A_{1}^{0}(x)\wedge A_{2}^{0}(x)\to B_{1}^{0}(x)\vee B_{2}^{0}(x);\\
A_{2}^{1}(x)&=&A_{1}^{0}(x)\wedge B_{1}^{0}(x)\to A_{2}^{0}(x)\vee B_{2}^{0}(x);\\
A_{3}^{1}(x)&=&A_{1}^{0}(x)\wedge B_{2}^{0}(x)\to A_{2}^{0}(x)\vee B_{1}^{0}(x);\\
B_{1}^{1}(x)&=&A_{2}^{0}(x)\wedge B_{1}^{0}(x)\to A_{1}^{0}(x)\vee B_{2}^{0}(x);\\
B_{2}^{1}(x)&=&A_{2}^{0}(x)\wedge B_{2}^{0}(x)\to A_{1}^{0}(x)\vee B_{1}^{0}(x);\\
B_{3}^{1}(x)&=&B_{1}^{0}(x)\wedge B_{2}^{0}(x)\to A_{1}^{0}(x)\vee A_{2}^{0}(x);\\
A_{1}^{2}(x)&=&A_{1}^{1}(x)\to B_{1}^{1}(x)\vee A_{2}^{1}(x)\vee B_{2}^{1}(x);\\
A_{2}^{2}(x)&=&A_{1}^{1}(x)\to B_{1}^{1}(x)\vee A_{2}^{1}(x)\vee B_{3}^{1}(x);\\
B_{1}^{2}(x)&=&B_{1}^{1}(x)\to A_{1}^{1}(x)\vee B_{2}^{1}(x)\vee A_{2}^{1}(x);\\
B_{2}^{2}(x)&=&B_{1}^{1}(x)\to A_{1}^{1}(x)\vee B_{2}^{1}(x)\vee A_{3}^{1}(x).\\
\end{array}

Next, consider the finite intuitionistic frame
\mathfrak{F}_{0}=\langle W_{0},R_{0}\rangle
, where

\begin{array}[]{lcl}W_{0}&=&\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta^{\prime}_{2},\delta_{3},\alpha^{0}_{1},\alpha^{0}_{2},\beta^{0}_{1},\beta^{0}_{2}\,\alpha^{1}_{1},\alpha^{1}_{2},\alpha^{1}_{3},\beta^{1}_{1},\beta^{1}_{2},\beta^{1}_{3},\alpha^{2}_{1},\alpha^{2}_{2},\beta^{2}_{1},\beta^{2}_{2}\}\end{array}

and
R_{0}
is reflexive transitive closure of the relation

\begin{array}[]{l}\{\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{0}_{1},\gamma_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{0}_{1},\gamma_{3}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{0}_{2},\gamma_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{0}_{2},\gamma_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{0}_{2},\gamma^{\prime}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{0}_{1},\gamma_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{0}_{1},\gamma^{\prime}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{0}_{1},\gamma_{3}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{0}_{2},\gamma_{1}\rangle$},\\
\phantom{\{}\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{0}_{2},\gamma_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{0}_{2},\gamma^{\prime}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{0}_{2},\gamma_{3}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{1}_{1},\alpha^{0}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{1}_{1},\beta^{0}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{1}_{1},\beta^{0}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{1}_{1},\beta^{0}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{1}_{2},\alpha^{0}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{1}_{2},\alpha^{0}_{2}\rangle$},\\
\phantom{\{}\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{1}_{3},\alpha^{0}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{1}_{3},\beta^{0}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{1}_{2},\alpha^{0}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{1}_{2},\alpha^{0}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{1}_{3},\alpha^{0}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{1}_{3},\alpha^{0}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{2}_{1},\beta^{1}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{2}_{1},\alpha^{1}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{2}_{1},\beta^{1}_{2}\rangle$},\\
\phantom{\{}\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{2}_{2},\beta^{1}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{2}_{2},\alpha^{1}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\alpha^{2}_{2},\beta^{1}_{3}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{2}_{1},\alpha^{1}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{2}_{1},\beta^{1}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{2}_{1},\alpha^{1}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{2}_{2},\alpha^{1}_{1}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{2}_{2},\beta^{1}_{2}\rangle$},\parbox{33.98851pt}{\hfill$\langle\beta^{2}_{2},\alpha^{1}_{3}\rangle$}\}.\end{array}

The frame is depicted in Figure
18
; the worlds are presented by white circles, the accessibility relation between the worlds is presented by arrows (we omit the arrows inferrable by reflexivity and transitivity).

An
a
-suitable model based on frame
\mathfrak{F}_{0}

Let
\mathcal{A}
be a set such that
|\mathcal{A}|\geqslant 3
and let
a
be an element of
\mathcal{A}
.
We call an intuitionistic model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\langle W_{0},R_{0}\rangle\odot\mathcal{A},I\rangle

a
-suitable
if there exists
a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a\}
such that, for every
w\in W_{0}
and every
b\in\mathcal{A}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}P^{\prime}(b)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mbox{either $w=\delta_{2}$ and $b\neq a$;}\\
&&\mbox{or $w\in\{\delta^{\prime}_{2},\beta_{1}^{0}\}$ and $b=a^{\prime}$;}\\
&&\mbox{or $w=\delta_{3}$ and $b\in\{a,a^{\prime}\}$,}\end{array}

see Figure
18
.

Lemma 5.23

Let
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\langle W_{0},R_{0}\rangle\odot\mathcal{A},I\rangle
be an
a
-suitable model. Then, for
every
w\in W_{0}
and all
k,m\in\{1,2\}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}{A_{m}^{k}}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&wR_{0}\alpha_{m}^{k};\\
\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}{B_{m}^{k}}(a)&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&wR_{0}\beta_{m}^{k}.\end{array}

A routine check. Also, the statement follows directly from
[
47
, Lemma 3.9]
.

Lemma 5.24

Let
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\langle W_{0},R_{0}\rangle\odot\mathcal{A},I\rangle
be an
a
-suitable model. Then, for
every
b\in\mathcal{A}\setminus\{a\}
, every
w\in W_{0}
, and every
m\in\{1,2\}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}{A_{m}^{2}}(b)&\mbox{and\/}&\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}{B_{m}^{2}}(b).\end{array}

A routine check. Also, the statement follows directly from
[
47
, Lemma 3.10]
.

Now, we are ready to simulate the letters
Q
and
G
in
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
by positive formulas that contain a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables. Let
S_{9}
be a function that replaces in formulas

•

each occurrence of
Q(x)
or
Q(y)
with
A^{2}_{1}(x)\vee B^{2}_{1}(x)
or
A^{2}_{1}(y)\vee B^{2}_{1}(y)
, respectively;

•

each occurrence of
G(x)
or
G(y)
with
A^{2}_{2}(x)\vee B^{2}_{2}(x)
or
A^{2}_{2}(y)\vee B^{2}_{2}(y)
, respectively.

Lemma 5.25

If
n\in\mathbb{X}
, then
S_{9}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\in\mathbf{QInt}
.

Follows from Lemma
5.17
, since
S_{9}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
is a substitution instance of
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
.

Lemma 5.26

Let
L\in\{\mathbf{QInt}_{\mathit{dfin}}+\bm{bd}_{7}+\bm{cd},\mathbf{QKC}_{\mathit{dfin}}+\bm{bd}_{8}+\bm{cd}\}
.
If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
S_{9}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in L
.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Y}
. Observe that
\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}(\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{3})
is an swKKZ class; then, by Lemma
5.18
,
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QSIL}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{dfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}(\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{3})
. Therefore, there are a Kripke frame
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
of depth at most
3
, a model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{F}\odot\mathcal{D},I\rangle
with a finite domain
\mathcal{D}
, and a world
w_{0}\in W
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
; without a loss of generality, we may assume that
|\mathcal{D}|\geqslant 3
.

For every
a\in\mathcal{D}
, let us take a copy
\mathfrak{F}_{0}^{a}=\langle W_{a},R_{a}\rangle
of
\mathfrak{F}_{0}
defined as follows:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}W_{a}&=&W_{0}\times\{a\};\\
R_{a}&=&\{\langle\langle v,a\rangle,\langle u,a\rangle\rangle:vR_{0}u\}.\end{array}

Define an intuitionistic Kripke frame
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}=\langle\bar{W},\bar{R}\rangle
by

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\bar{W}&=&\displaystyle W\cup{\bigcup\{W_{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}}\\
\end{array}

and
\bar{R}
be the reflexive transitive closure of the relation

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\displaystyle R\cup\bigcup\{R_{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}\cup\{\langle w,\langle\alpha^{2}_{1},a\rangle\rangle,\langle w,\langle\beta^{2}_{1},a\rangle\rangle:\mbox{$w\in W$, $a\in\mathcal{D}$, $\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}Q(a)$}\}\\
\displaystyle\phantom{R\cup\bigcup\{R_{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}}{}\cup\{\langle w,\langle\alpha^{2}_{2},a\rangle\rangle,\langle w,\langle\beta^{2}_{2},a\rangle\rangle:\mbox{$w\in W$, $a\in\mathcal{D}$, $\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}G(a)$}\}\\
\displaystyle\phantom{R\cup\bigcup\{R_{a}:a\in\mathcal{D}\}}{}\cup\{\langle w,\langle\alpha^{1}_{1},a\rangle\rangle,\langle w,\langle\beta^{1}_{1},a\rangle\rangle:\mbox{$w\in W$, $a\in\mathcal{D}$}\}.\\
\end{array}

Observe that
\mathfrak{F}_{0}^{a}
is a subframe of
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}
, for every
a\in\mathcal{D}
.
Let
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}=\langle\bar{\mathfrak{F}}\odot\mathcal{D},\bar{I}\rangle
be a model such that

•

for every
w\in W
,
\bar{I}(P^{\prime})=\varnothing
;

•

for every
a\in\mathcal{D}
, its submodel
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{a}=\langle\mathfrak{F}_{0}^{a}\odot\mathcal{D},\bar{I}_{a}\rangle
, where
\bar{I}_{a}(v,P^{\prime})=\bar{I}(v,P^{\prime})
for every
v\in W_{a}
, is
a
-suitable.

Let us make some useful observations about model
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
(cf.
[
47
, Lemma 3.11]
).

Sublemma 5.27

If
w\in W
and
a\in\mathcal{D}
, then
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{1}_{1}(a)\vee B^{1}_{1}(a)
.

Notice that
w\bar{R}\langle\alpha^{1}_{1},a\rangle
and
w\bar{R}\langle\beta^{1}_{1},a\rangle
by the definition of
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}
; then apply Lemma
5.23
(and heredity), since
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{a}
is
a
-suitable.

Sublemma 5.28

If
\varphi
is a positive formula whose predicate letters are in
\{Q,G\}
and individual variables are in
\{x,y\}
,
v\in\bar{W}\setminus W
, and
g
is an assignment in
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
, then
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},v\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}S_{9}\varphi
.

By Lemmas
5.23
and
5.24
,
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{a}\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{2}_{k}(b)\vee B^{2}_{k}(b)
, for every
k\in\{1,2\}
and all
a,b\in\mathcal{D}
. Then apply induction on
\varphi
(taking into account that
\varphi
is positive).

Sublemma 5.29

For every positive formula
\varphi
whose predicate letters are in
\{Q,G\}
and individual variables are in
\{x,y\}
, every
w\in W
, and every assignment
g
in
\mathfrak{M}
,

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathfrak{M},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}\varphi&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}S_{9}\varphi.\end{array}

Induction on
\varphi
.

Let
\varphi
be
Q(x)
or
Q(y)
.

If
\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}Q(a)
, for some
w\in W
and
a\in\mathcal{D}
, then
w\bar{R}\langle\alpha^{2}_{1},a\rangle
and
w\bar{R}\langle\beta^{2}_{1},a\rangle
. It follows from Lemma
5.23
that
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{a},\langle\alpha^{2}_{1},a\rangle\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{2}_{1}(a)
and
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{a},\langle\alpha^{2}_{1},a\rangle\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}B^{2}_{1}(a)
, since
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}_{a}
is
a
-suitable. Then, by heredity,

\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{2}_{1}(a)\vee B^{2}_{1}(a)
.

Suppose that
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{2}_{1}(a)\vee B^{2}_{1}(a)
, for some
w\in W
and
a\in\mathcal{D}
.
Then
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{2}_{1}(a)
and
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}B^{2}_{1}(a)
; hence, there exist
u,v\in\bar{R}(w)
such that

\begin{array}[]{llll}\bar{\mathfrak{M}},u\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{1}_{1}(a),&\bar{\mathfrak{M}},u\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}B^{1}_{1}(a),&\bar{\mathfrak{M}},u\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{1}_{2}(a),&\bar{\mathfrak{M}},u\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}B^{1}_{2}(a);\\
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},v\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}B^{1}_{1}(a),&\bar{\mathfrak{M}},v\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{1}_{1}(a),&\bar{\mathfrak{M}},v\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}B^{1}_{2}(a),&\bar{\mathfrak{M}},v\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{1}_{2}(a).\end{array}

By Sublemma
5.27
,
u,v\not\in W
. Observe that
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},u\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}A^{2}_{1}(a)
and
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},v\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}B^{2}_{1}(a)
; hence, by Sublemma
5.28
,
u,v\not\in W_{b}
with
b\neq a
. Thus,
u,v\in W_{a}
. Then, Lemma
5.23
implies that
u=\langle\alpha^{2}_{1},a\rangle
and
v=\langle\beta^{2}_{1},a\rangle
. By the definition of
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
, this is possible only if
\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}Q(a)
.

Let
\varphi
be
G(x)
or
G(y)
. Then use a similar argumentation.

Let
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\to\varphi^{\prime\prime}
with the statement of the sublemma being true for
\varphi^{\prime}
and
\varphi^{\prime\prime}
.

If
\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}\varphi
, for some
w\in W
and some assignment
g
, then
\mathfrak{M},w^{\prime}\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}\varphi^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{M},w^{\prime}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}\varphi^{\prime\prime}
, for some
w^{\prime}\in R(w)
. Then

\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w^{\prime}\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}S_{9}\varphi^{\prime}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w^{\prime}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}S_{9}\varphi^{\prime\prime}
; hence,
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}S_{9}\varphi
.

Suppose that
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}S_{9}\varphi
, for some
w\in W
and some assignment
g
. Then
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w^{\prime}\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}S_{9}\varphi^{\prime}
and
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w^{\prime}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}S_{9}\varphi^{\prime\prime}
, for some
w^{\prime}\in\bar{R}(w)
. By Sublemma
5.28
,
w^{\prime}\in W
. Then
\mathfrak{M},w^{\prime}\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}\varphi^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{M},w^{\prime}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}\varphi^{\prime\prime}
; hence,
\mathfrak{M},w\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}^{g}\varphi
.

The cases
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\wedge\varphi^{\prime\prime}
,
\varphi=\varphi^{\prime}\vee\varphi^{\prime\prime}
,
\varphi=\forall z\,\varphi^{\prime}
, and
\varphi=\exists z\,\varphi^{\prime}
, where
z\in\{x,y\}
, are similar and left to the reader.

By Sublemma
5.29
,
\bar{\mathfrak{M}},w_{0}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}S_{9}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
. Observe that
\bar{\mathfrak{F}}
is of depth at most
7
. Thus,
S_{9}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QInt}_{\mathit{dfin}}+\bm{bd}_{7}+\bm{cd}
.

In order to prove that
S_{9}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathbf{QKC}_{\mathit{dfin}}+\bm{bd}_{8}+\bm{cd}
, just extend
\bar{\mathfrak{M}}
with a new world accessible from all the worlds of the model and then make the formula
\forall x\,P^{\prime}(x)
being true at this world.

Lemma 5.30

Let
L\in\{\mathbf{QInt}_{\mathit{wfin}}+\bm{bd}_{7}+\bm{cd},\mathbf{QKC}_{\mathit{wfin}}+\bm{bd}_{8}+\bm{cd}\}
.
If
n\in\mathbb{Y}
, then
S_{9}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in L
.

Similarly to the proof of Lemma
5.26
. We give just some comments.

Let
n\in\mathbb{Y}
. Observe that
\mathop{\mbox{{{fin}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}(\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{3})
is an swKKZ class; then, by Lemma
5.19
,
S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}\not\in\mathop{\mbox{{{QSIL}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{wfin}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}(\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{3})
. Therefore, there are a finite Kripke frame
\mathfrak{F}=\langle W,R\rangle
of depth at most
3
, a model
\mathfrak{M}=\langle\mathfrak{F}\odot\mathcal{D},I\rangle
with a domain
\mathcal{D}
, and a world
w_{0}\in W
such that
\mathfrak{M},w_{0}\not\mathrel{|\hskip-1.25pt|\hskip-2.8pt{-}}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
.

Observe that we may assume
\mathcal{D}
being finite (and, additionally, with
|\mathcal{D}|\geqslant 3
); we can extract this from Proposition
4.19
or from the proof of Lemma
5.19
. Then, applying the same argumentation as in the proof of Lemma
5.26
, we refute
S_{9}S_{8}\mathit{M}^{\mathit{int}}\mathit{Tiling}_{n}^{\mathbb{X}}
in a finite intuitionistic model of the depth required, and similarly for
\mathbf{QKC}
; we leave the details to the reader.

Theorem 5.31

The positive fragments of logics

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\mathbf{QInt}&\mbox{and\/}&\mathbf{QInt}_{\mathit{dfin}}\hfill+\bm{bd}_{7}+\bm{cd},\\
\mathbf{QInt}&\mbox{and\/}&\mathbf{QInt}_{\mathit{wfin}}\hfill+\bm{bd}_{7}+\bm{cd},\\
\mathbf{QInt}&\mbox{and\/}&\mathbf{QKC}_{\mathit{dfin}}\hfill+\bm{bd}_{8}+\bm{cd},\\
\mathbf{QInt}&\mbox{and\/}&\mathbf{QKC}_{\mathit{wfin}}\hfill+\bm{bd}_{8}+\bm{cd}\end{array}

are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables.

Follows from Lemmas
5.25
,
5.26
, and
5.30
.

Corollary 5.32

Let
\mathbf{QInt}\subseteq L\subseteq L^{\prime}
and also either
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{7}+\bm{cd}
or
L^{\prime}\subseteq\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{8}+\bm{cd}
. Then the positive fragments of
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{wfin}}
as well as
L
and
L^{\prime}_{\mathit{dfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables.

Corollary 5.33

Let
L
be one of
\mathbf{QInt}
,
\mathbf{QKP}
,
\mathbf{QKC}
,
\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{n}
with
n\geqslant 7
,
\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{n}
with
n\geqslant 8
. Then the positive fragments of
L
and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
as well as
L
and
L_{\mathit{dfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables.

6
Computational complexity

6.1
Lower bounds for monadic fragments

Notice that the technique proving the recursive inseparability of fragments of modal and superintuitionistic logics also provides us with lower bounds for computational complexity of the fragments. We can argue similar to Section
3.5
. We omit the argumentation and only give the resulting statements.

For modal predicate logics, we obtain the following statements.

Theorem 6.1

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QK}
and is contained in at least one of the logics
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QGL.3.bf}
,
\mathbf{QGrz.3.bf}
,
\mathbf{QGL.bf}\oplus\bm{bd}_{2}
,
\mathbf{QGrz.bf}\oplus\bm{bd}_{2}
. Then the fragment of
L
in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard.

Theorem 6.2

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QK}
and is contained in at least one of the logics
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QGL.3}
,
\mathbf{QGrz.3}
,
\mathbf{QGL}\oplus\bm{bd}_{2}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}\oplus\bm{bd}_{2}
. Then the fragments of
L_{\mathit{dfin}}
,
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
L_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
, and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard.

Notice that Theorems
6.1
and
6.2
remain true if we additionally require that the language does not contain
\bot
(and hence,
\neg
and
\Diamond
), see Remark
4.18
.

For superintuitionistic predicate logics, we obtain similar statements.

Theorem 6.3

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QInt}
and is contained in either
\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{2}+\bm{cd}
or
\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{3}+\bm{cd}
. Then the positive fragment of
L
in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard.

Theorem 6.4

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QInt}
and is contained in either
\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{2}+\bm{cd}
or
\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{3}+\bm{cd}
. Then the positive fragments of
L_{\mathit{dfin}}
,
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
L_{\mathit{dfin}}+\bm{cd}
, and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}+\bm{cd}
in the language with a single unary predicate letter and three individual variables are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard.

Of course, the fragments of such logics as
\mathbf{QInt}
,
\mathbf{QKP}
,
\mathbf{QKC}
,
\mathbf{QK}
,
\mathbf{QT}
,
\mathbf{QD}
,
\mathbf{QKB}
,
\mathbf{QKTB}
,
\mathbf{QK4}
,
\mathbf{QS4}
,
\mathbf{QK5}
,
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
,
\mathbf{QK4B}
,
\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}
,
\mathbf{QwGrz}
, etc. are
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete, since the logics are recursively enumerable. Observe that the classes of their finite Kripke frames are also recursively enumerable, therefore, the monadic fragments of
\mathbf{QInt}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QKP}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QKC}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QK}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QT}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QD}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QKB}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QKTB}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QK4}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QS4}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QK5}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QK45}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QK4B}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QGL}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QwGrz}_{\mathit{wfin}}
, etc. are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-complete, see
[
49
, Theorem 4.4]
.

Similar results are true for the monadic fragments of logics with two individual variables in the language. Notice that we then loose some logics.

Theorem 6.5

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QK}
and is contained in at least one of the logics
\mathbf{QGL.bf}\oplus\bm{bd}_{4}
,
\mathbf{QGrz.bf}\oplus\bm{bd}_{4}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz.bf}\oplus\bm{bd}_{3}
. Then the fragment of
L
in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables is
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard.

Theorem 6.6

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QK}
and is contained in at least one of the logics
\mathbf{QGL.bf}\oplus\bm{bd}_{4}
,
\mathbf{QGrz.bf}\oplus\bm{bd}_{4}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz.bf}\oplus\bm{bd}_{3}
. Then the fragments of
L_{\mathit{dfin}}
,
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
L_{\mathit{dfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
, and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard.

Theorem 6.7

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QInt}
and is contained in either
\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{7}+\bm{cd}
or
\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{8}+\bm{cd}
. Then the positive fragment of
L
in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables is

-hard.

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QInt}
and is contained in either
\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{7}+\bm{cd}
or
\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{8}+\bm{cd}
. Then the positive fragments of

,

,

, and

in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables are

-hard.

6.2
Lower bounds for dyadic fragments

The situation with lower bounds for computational complexity of dyadic fragments differs from the one for monadic fragments. Let us make some observations.

Proposition 6.9

Let
L
be a modal predicate logic containing
\mathbf{QK}
and contained in at least one of the logics
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QGL.3}
,
\mathbf{QGrz.3}
,
\mathbf{QGL}\oplus\bm{bd}_{2}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}\oplus\bm{bd}_{2}
. Then the fragments of
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}\oplus\bm{bf}
in the language with a single binary predicate letter and three individual variables are both
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard and
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard.

Indeed,
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hardness follows from the Church Theorem (or from the Trakhtenbrot Theorem, see Theorem
3.7
) and
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hardness follows from Theorem
6.2
, since we can simulate
Q(x)
by
P(x,x)
.

Proposition 6.10

Let
L
be a superintuitionistic predicate logic that contains
\mathbf{QInt}
and is contained in either
\mathbf{QInt}+\bm{bd}_{2}+\bm{cd}
or
\mathbf{QKC}+\bm{bd}_{3}+\bm{cd}
. Then the positive fragments of
L_{\mathit{wfin}}
and
L_{\mathit{wfin}}+\bm{cd}
in the language with a single binary predicate letter and three individual variables are both
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard and
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard.

Again,
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hardness follows from the Church Theorem (or Theorem
3.7
) with use of the Kolmogorov embedding (or some similar) and
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hardness follows from Theorem
6.3
.

6.3
Remarks on high undecidability

The technique presented here makes it possible to prove only
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hardness or
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hardness of fragments of logics. However, the monadic fragments of some logics are not contained in either
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
or
\Pi^{0}_{1}
. We give some examples.

For a modal predicate logic
L
, let us define
20
20

20

In general, it is more appropriate to define Kripke completion of
L
as the logic of the class of e-augmented frames of
L
; the use of this notion will be such that we will not feel the difference.
its
Kripke completion

L^{\mathit{K}}
as the logic of the class of Kripke frames validating
L
,
i.e.,
L^{\mathit{K}}=\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{\mathit{e}}}_{\mathit{\mathit{all}}}}\mathop{\mbox{{{KF}}}}L
.
Let us consider logics
\mathbf{QGL}^{\mathit{K}}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}^{\mathit{K}}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz}^{\mathit{K}}
.
It is known
[
27
,
32
,
31
]
that
\mathbf{QGL}^{\mathit{K}}\neq\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}^{\mathit{K}}\neq\mathbf{QGrz}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz}^{\mathit{K}}\neq\mathbf{QwGrz}
, i.e.,
\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz}
are Kripke incomplete. Using an estimate of computational complexity, we shall show this along the way, even for the monadic fragments; but our main goal is to draw attention to how high the lower estimates for the computational complexity of
\mathbf{QGL}^{\mathit{K}}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}^{\mathit{K}}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz}^{\mathit{K}}
are.

Logics
\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz}
are recursively enumerable; since they are KHC-friendly, their monadic fragments are
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard, and hence
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-complete.
The monadic fragments of
\mathbf{QGL}_{\mathit{wfin}}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}_{\mathit{wfin}}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz}_{\mathit{wfin}}
are
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-complete
[
49
, Theorem 4.4]
.
As for the monadic fragments of
\mathbf{QGL}^{\mathit{K}}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}^{\mathit{K}}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz}^{\mathit{K}}
, they are
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hard, since the logics are KHC-friendly. But the fragments are also
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hard
[
33
]
and even
\Pi^{1}_{1}
-hard in the language with two unary predicate letters and two individual variables
[
31
, Theorem 3]
. As a corollary, indeed, the monadic fragments of
\mathbf{QGL}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}
, and
\mathbf{QwGrz}
are Kripke incomplete.

Thus, the lower bounds for the computational complexity of the monadic fragments of some “natural” KHC-friendly modal predicate logics can be much higher than those that can be obtained using the theorems of Section
6.1
.
Near examples can be found in
[
45
,
46
]
; in particular, for
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{e}}_{\mathit{all}}}\langle\mathds{N},\leqslant\rangle
and
\mathop{\mbox{{{QML}}}^{\mathit{c}}_{\mathit{all}}}\langle\mathds{N},\leqslant\rangle
.

7
Conclusion

7.1
Remarks and generalizations

Notice that the methods and the results presented here can be quite easily transferred to some other classes of predicate logics; let us mention some of them here.

The “classical” part of the results is expandable to the free logic and its theories. This logic rejects the law
\forall x\,P(x)\to\exists x\,P(x)
but this feature does not affect the construction described. Also, we can apply the construction to logics and theories in the languages enriched with some non-elementary tools (for example, the transitive closure operator); in this case sometimes two individual variables are sufficient to obtain similar results
[
14
,
38
,
39
]
and even to prove
\Sigma^{1}_{1}
-hardness of the satisfiability problem.

The “modal” part can be easily expanded to polymodal logics, to logics defined via varying domain semantics, and to non-normal modal logics (which are not extensions of logic
\mathbf{QK}
). Moreover, in polymodal logics, it is possible to refine some results obtained for their monomodal counterparts. For example, in
\mathbf{QS5}_{2}
with modalities
\Box_{1}
and
\Box_{2}
, we can define the modality
\Box_{1}\Box_{2}
; clearly, on Kripke frames it corresponds to the relations being reflexive but not necessarily symmetric or transitive. So, the logic of its finite Kripke frames is undecidable in the language with two individual variables and a single unary predicate letter. In contrast, to prove the undecidability of
\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{wfin}}
, we used either three individual variables or three unary predicate letters.

The “intuitionistic” part can be transferred in a straightforward way on logics with near semantics as well. For example, on predicate counterparts of basic propositional logic and formal propositional logic introduced by A. Visser
[
66
]
. A distinctive feature of the Kripke semantics for them is that it allows irreflexive worlds for the former logic and requires irreflexivity
21
21

21

More exactly, the Kripke frames for the logic are exactly strict Noetherian orders.
for the latter one. However, constructions similar to the one described here can work for it with slight technical modifications
[
43
]
.

Also, observe that the results on recursive inseparability allow us to obtain then undecidability as
\Sigma^{0}_{1}
-hardness or
\Pi^{0}_{1}
-hardness only. As we have seen, some logics have higher complexity, even in languages with two-three individual variables and one-two unary predicate letters
[
45
,
46
,
31
]
. So, the results for them, similar to those presented here, may appear too weak and, in some cases, could be improved.

7.3
Future work

There are several questions related to the algorithmic complexity of fragments of predicate logics that remain unanswered so far. It seems that the solutions to some of them are a matter of technique, but for some of them, apparently, this is not the case.

Let us start with
\mathbf{QS5}
and near logics such as
\mathbf{QK5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
, and
\mathbf{QK4B}
. Our results do not give an answer on the decidability of
\mathbf{QS5}
if its language contains only two individual variables and one or two unary predicate letters. However, as we have seen,
\mathbf{QS5}
with three individual variables and a single unary predicate letter is undecidable, and the same for two individual variables and three unary predicate letters. It seems that one of three unary predicate letters can be eliminated; but all attempts of the author to do this have so far failed.

Conjecture 7.1

The fragment of
\mathbf{QS5}
in the language with two individual variables and two unary predicate letters is algorithmically undecidable.

As for two variables and a single unary predicate letter, the fragment of
\mathbf{QS5}
in this language seems to be decidable. Moreover, it seems to the author that the fragment is finitely approximable, in particular,
\mathbf{QS5}
,
\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{dfin}}
, and
\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{wfin}}
do not differ by formulas of the fragment.

Conjecture 7.2

The fragment of
\mathbf{QS5}
in the language with two individual variables and a single unary predicate letter is algorithmically decidable.

In both conjectures,
\mathbf{QS5}
can be replaced with
\mathbf{QK5}
,
\mathbf{QK45}
,
\mathbf{QKD45}
or
\mathbf{QK4B}
. Also observe that if one of the conjectures is wrong, then so is the other.

Next, let us concern the logics of Kripke frames of bounded depth. What happens if we bound the depth of frames by a quite small number? For example, what can we say about the decidability of the fragment of
\mathbf{QK}\oplus\bm{bd}_{2}
in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables? What about similar fragments of
\mathbf{QGL}\oplus\bm{bd}_{3}
,
\mathbf{QGrz}\oplus\bm{bd}_{3}
,
\mathbf{QInt}\oplus\bm{bd}_{6}
or
\mathbf{QKC}\oplus\bm{bd}_{7}
? It seems to the author that at least some of them are undecidable.

Another class we would like to talk about is the class of predicate logics defined by frames of bounded width. There are known results showing that such logics can be undecidable — and even highly undecidable — in languages with one-two unary predicate letters and two-three individual variables. It would be interesting to expand the ideas presented here on such logics in a regular way.

Finally, let us turn to the semantics used.
To obtain the results presented here, we did not use all v-augmented frames but only e-augmented and c-augmented ones. However, v-augmented frames allow us to distinguish more subtle properties described by modal predicate formulas. For example, the
converse Barcan formula

\text{{{cbf}}}=\Box\forall x\,P(x)\to\forall x\,\Box P(x)
is valid on the class e-augmented frames (and hence, on the class of c-augmented ones) but it is refuted on a v-augmented frame containing a world seeing a different world. It seems that the results obtained and the methods used should be transferred to this semantics, however, this requires a separate study.

In general, it is known that Kripke semantics is a rather weak tool for investigating predicate non-classical logics. There are a lot of results showing that predicate counterparts of Kripke complete propositional logics can be (and often are) Kripke incomplete. Of course, the technique presented here uses mainly soundness, not Kripke completeness; nevertheless, it seems reasonable to expect that using other semantics will yield more general results.

8
Note

After the paper was prepared, the author obtained a result from which it follows that Conjecture
7.1
is true, but Conjecture
7.2
is not. The result is presented in the following theorem.

Theorem 8.1

Logics
\mathbf{QK}
and
\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{wfin}}
as well as
\mathbf{QK}
and
\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{dfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables.

The proof is based on similar methods, but requires a different modeling; the author suggests presenting it in a separate paper.

In Theorem
8.1
, logic
\mathbf{QK}
can be replaced with any logic between
\mathbf{QK}
and
\mathbf{QS5}
; so, let us replace it with
\mathbf{QS5}
in order to deduce one interesting corollary.

Theorem 8.2

Logics
\mathbf{QS5}
and
\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{wfin}}
as well as
\mathbf{QK}
and
\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{dfin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with a single unary predicate letter and two individual variables.

Note that the atomic formulas
Q(x)
and
Q(y)
can be modeled in classical predicate logic by formulas
P(x,z)
and
P(y,z)
, respectively. The intended meaning of
P(x,z)
and
P(y,z)
is that
Q(x)
and
Q(y)
are true in a world
z
. Let us define the following translation
\mathit{ST}
of the modal formulas built from
Q(x)
and
Q(y)
into the classical ones:

\begin{array}[]{lcll}\mathit{ST}(\bot)&=&\bot;\\
\mathit{ST}(Q(v))&=&P(v,z),&\mbox{where $v\in\{x,y\}$};\\
\mathit{ST}(\varphi\wedge\psi)&=&\mathit{ST}(\varphi)\wedge\mathit{ST}(\psi);\\
\mathit{ST}(\varphi\vee\psi)&=&\mathit{ST}(\varphi)\vee\mathit{ST}(\psi);\\
\mathit{ST}(\varphi\to\psi)&=&\mathit{ST}(\varphi)\to\mathit{ST}(\psi);\\
\mathit{ST}(\Box\varphi)&=&\forall z\,\mathit{ST}(\varphi);\\
\mathit{ST}(\exists v\,\varphi)&=&\exists v\,\mathit{ST}(\varphi),&\mbox{where $v\in\{x,y\}$};\\
\mathit{ST}(\forall v\,\varphi)&=&\forall v\,\mathit{ST}(\varphi),&\mbox{where $v\in\{x,y\}$}.\\
\end{array}

In fact,
\mathit{ST}
is the standard translation, see
[
64
,
5
,
12
]
, adapted to
\mathbf{QS5}
; so, the following statement should be obvious.

Lemma 8.3

For every
\mathcal{ML}
-formula
\varphi
with a single unary predicate letter
Q
and individual variables
x
and
y
, the following equivalences hold:

\begin{array}[]{lcl}\varphi\in\mathbf{QS5}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mathit{ST}(\varphi)\in\mathbf{QCl};\\
\varphi\in\mathbf{QS5}_{\mathit{wfin}}&\mathrel{\Longleftrightarrow}&\mathit{ST}(\varphi)\in\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}.\\
\end{array}

Then, we obtain a refinement of the Trakhtenbrot Theorem for the fragment of
\mathcal{L}
defined by the atomic formulas
P(x,z)
and
P(y,z)
.

Theorem 8.4

Logics
\mathbf{QCl}
and
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
are recursively inseparable in the language with
P(x,z)
and
P(y,z)
as the only atomic formulas.

Immediately follows from Theorem
8.2
and Lemma
8.3
.

At the time of writing, the author does not know whether it is possible to replace
\mathbf{QCl}_{\mathit{fin}}
with
\mathbf{SIB}_{\mathit{fin}}
or
\mathbf{SRB}_{\mathit{fin}}
in Theorem
8.4
.
